## 303   DISCUSSION

304   In this study, we tested the preference‐performance relationship for three generalist [SOURCE: Page_104]

305   parasitoids on twelve aphid species and used previous results on specialist parasitoids. We [SOURCE: Page_104]

306   showed no relationship for A. abdominalis and D. rapae while a significant relationship was [SOURCE: Page_104]

307   found for A. ervi. Overall, the three parasitoid species showed a low behavioral selectivity [SOURCE: Page_104]

308   (preference traits) whereas their performances, and specifically for A. ervi, were strongly [SOURCE: Page_104]

309   linked to actual suitability for offspring development in the parasitized host species. The two [SOURCE: Page_104]

310   secondary endosymbionts H. defensa and R. insecticola were detected in only two host [SOURCE: Page_104]

311   species (M. dirhodum and A. fabae) and the host specificity index indicated that D. rapae [SOURCE: Page_104]

312   and A. abdominalis are true generalist parasitoids whereas A. ervi and L. testaceipes are [SOURCE: Page_104]

313   likely intermediate specialist‐generalist, and B. communis and B. koreanus are specialist [SOURCE: Page_104]

314   parasitoids (Desneux et al. 2009a, 2009b, unpublished data). [SOURCE: Page_104]

315   Preference traits [SOURCE: Page_104]

316   As expected for generalist parasitoids, the three species tested showed low host selectivity, [SOURCE: Page_104]

317   and they stung mostly all aphid species encountered at high rates. However, preference was [SOURCE: Page_104]

318   modulated by different factors (1) A. abdominalis and A. ervi had less stung the black aphids [SOURCE: Page_104]

319   compared to the other aphid colors (respectively, X 23 = 49.44; 69.4, dispersion parameter = [SOURCE: Page_104]

320   4.3; 7.1, P < 0.02), (2) A. abdominalis and A. ervi stung mainly the aphids from the [SOURCE: Page_104]

321   macrosiphini tribe (respectively, X 21 = 31.86; 55.36, dispersion parameter = 4.9; 7.75, P < [SOURCE: Page_104]

97 [SOURCE: Page_104]

322   0.01) and (3) A. abdominalis had less stung the aphids on squash (X 23 = 60.5, dispersion [SOURCE: Page_105]

323   parameter = 4.32, P = 0.03). The presence of endosymbiont did not modulate the parasitoid [SOURCE: Page_105]

324   preference (respectively for A. abdominalis, A. ervi and D. rapae X 21 = 14.24; 2.21; 32.9, [SOURCE: Page_105]

325   dispersion parameter = 6.88; 10.9; 11.6, P > 0.05). Aphid defensive behaviors are known to [SOURCE: Page_105]

326   potentially affect oviposition behavior of various aphid parasitoids and reduce the parasitoid [SOURCE: Page_105]

327   host range (Kouamé and Mackauer 1991, Wyckhuys et al. 2008, Desneux et al. 2009a). In [SOURCE: Page_105]

328   line with these studies, no relationship was found between aphid stung rates by A. ervi and [SOURCE: Page_105]

329   D. rapae and the proportion of aphid defenses observed, suggesting that their ‘quick’ sting [SOURCE: Page_105]

330   syndrome avoid aphid defenses (Desneux et al. 2009c, Bilodeau et al. 2013,). By contrast, [SOURCE: Page_105]

331   there was a negative relationship between the proportion of aphid species stung by A. [SOURCE: Page_105]

332   abdominalis and the proportion of aphid defensive behaviors. Aphelinus abdominalis [SOURCE: Page_105]

333   showed stinging time ranging between 20 and 60 seconds, so about four times more than [SOURCE: Page_105]

334   the two other parasitoid species tested. This behavioral pattern suggests that aphid [SOURCE: Page_105]

335   defensive behaviors may disturb females during their stinging event (De Farias and Hopper [SOURCE: Page_105]

336   1999, Wahab 1985). Secondly, the success of aphid defenses depends on the relative size of [SOURCE: Page_105]

337   attacking parasitoid vs. aphid. A. abdominalis is two times smaller than A. ervi and D. rapae [SOURCE: Page_105]

338   which may attack aphids more easily (Le Ralec et al. 2010). Finally, A. abdominalis did not [SOURCE: Page_105]

339   show a particular behavior enable to lower occurrence of aphid defenses, as observed in [SOURCE: Page_105]

340   several other aphid parasitoid species, e.g. antennal tapping mimicking the behavior of [SOURCE: Page_105]

341   aphid‐mutualistic ants (Völkl and Kroupa 1997). [SOURCE: Page_105]

342   Performance traits [SOURCE: Page_105]

343   The three generalist parasitoids showed higher host specificity index values compared to the [SOURCE: Page_105]

344   specialized species considered in this assessment. Indeed, Binodoxys communis and B. [SOURCE: Page_105]

98 [SOURCE: Page_105]

345   koreanus are specialized on the Aphis genus (Desneux et al. 2009 a, b) whereas L. [SOURCE: Page_106]

346   testaceipes is specialized on the Aphidini tribe (Desneux & Heimpel, unpublished data, figure [SOURCE: Page_106]

347   4). By contrast, A. abdominalis and D. rapae had a successful development in both tested [SOURCE: Page_106]

348   tribes and in different genus tested. However, A. ervi showed a lower host specificity index [SOURCE: Page_106]

349   than the two other generalist species because its performance was more efficient on host [SOURCE: Page_106]

350   from Macrosiphini tribe (Zepeda‐paulo et al. 2013, Raymond et al. 2015), indicating that A. [SOURCE: Page_106]

351   ervi could be considered as an intermediate specialist‐generalist, i.e. it has the same host [SOURCE: Page_106]

352   range than a generalist one but differ in their relative performance (Klinken et al. 2000). [SOURCE: Page_106]

353   Different physiological and ecological factors could give aphid resistance against immature [SOURCE: Page_106]

354   parasitoids and modify parasitoid host range. The primary sources of resistance are poor [SOURCE: Page_106]

355   parasitoid ability to control the host metabolism, the presence of secondary endosymbionts [SOURCE: Page_106]

356   in aphids, and the aphid ability to sequester toxic compounds and/or the host quality itself. [SOURCE: Page_106]

357   Generalist organisms have a successful development in a wide host range through [SOURCE: Page_106]

358   phenotypic plasticity, i.e. the ability to express different phenotypes depending on the [SOURCE: Page_106]

359   environment (Agrawal 2001) and transgenerational phenotypic plasticity, i.e. the offspring [SOURCE: Page_106]

360   would be primed to develop in the host in which their mother developed (Mousseau and [SOURCE: Page_106]

361   Fox 1998). The generalist behavior of the three parasitoid species in association with their [SOURCE: Page_106]

362   phenotypic plasticity may rapidly develop an adaptation to novel host species (Fox and [SOURCE: Page_106]

363   Mousseau 1998). However, becoming acclimated to its host may be costly for the [SOURCE: Page_106]

364   parasitoids (Uller 2008). [SOURCE: Page_106]

365   The facultative endosymbionts present in aphids may compromise the successful [SOURCE: Page_106]

366   development of parasitoids and reduce their host ranges (Ferrari et al. 2004, Oliver et al. [SOURCE: Page_106]

367   2005, McLean and Godfray 2015). Specifically, Hamiltonella defensa and Regiella insecticola [SOURCE: Page_106]

99 [SOURCE: Page_106]

368   are known to protect aphids against different natural enemies such as parasitoids (Oliver et [SOURCE: Page_107]

369   al. 2003, von Burg et al. 2008). In this study, A. abdominalis was not impacted by the [SOURCE: Page_107]

370   presence of secondary endosymbionts in aphids and had 84% of successful parasitism in M. [SOURCE: Page_107]

371   dirhodum. The impact of H. defensa in the D. rapae development cannot be discussed [SOURCE: Page_107]

372   because A. fabae and M. dirhodum are low parasitized. However, a high parasitoid larval [SOURCE: Page_107]

373   mortality of A. ervi was observed when it encountered A. fabae and M. dirhodum, whereas [SOURCE: Page_107]

374   H. defensa was detected in A. fabae and M. dirhodum and R. insecticola was also detected in [SOURCE: Page_107]

375   M. dirhodum (as reported by Henry et al. 2015). However, A. ervi is well known to parasite [SOURCE: Page_107]

376   Macrosiphininae species such as A. pisum, M. euphorbiae, S. avenae and M. dirhodum (Stary [SOURCE: Page_107]

377   et al. 1993, Kavallieratos et al. 2004), suggesting that the presence of H. defensa and/or R. [SOURCE: Page_107]

378   insecticola reduces the A. ervi host range. Several studies have shown that endosymbionts [SOURCE: Page_107]

379   confer protection only against the more specialized natural enemies and not against [SOURCE: Page_107]

380   generalist ones (Parker et al. 2013, Asplen et al. 2014, Hrcek et al. 2016, Kraft et al. 2017), [SOURCE: Page_107]

381   which consolidate the hypothesis whereby A. ervi is an intermediate specialist‐generalist. [SOURCE: Page_107]

382   Aphid ability to sequester toxic secondary metabolites when feeding on toxic plants [SOURCE: Page_107]

383   (Mooney et al. 2008, Pratt et al. 2008) might also impact the parasitoid host range (Helms et [SOURCE: Page_107]

384   al. 2004). The specialist aphid species A. nerii and B. brassicae are known to sequester [SOURCE: Page_107]

385   cardenolide (Asclepias) and glucosinolate (cabbage) respectively (Desneux et al. 2009a, [SOURCE: Page_107]

386   Jones et al. 2001) and these toxic allelochemical molecules have a drastic impact on [SOURCE: Page_107]

387   immature parasitoid survival (Mooney et al. 2008, Pratt et al. 2008, Desneux et al. 2009a, [SOURCE: Page_107]

388   Kos et al. 2011). The three generalist parasitoids cannot successfully parasitize A. nerii [SOURCE: Page_107]

389   despite a high sting rate (ranged from 0.55 to 0.84). They all reached the larval stage and [SOURCE: Page_107]

390   then died. Similarly, A. abdominalis and A. ervi cannot successfully parasitize B. brassicae [SOURCE: Page_107]

100 [SOURCE: Page_107]

391   despite a high sting rate (0.73 and 0.56 respectively). However, D. rapae is well known to [SOURCE: Page_108]

392   have a successful development in B. brassicae; suggesting the success of the acclimation [SOURCE: Page_108]

393   through phenotypic plasticity for this parasitoid. By contrast, M. persicae is not a [SOURCE: Page_108]

394   glucosinolate‐sequestering aphid whether it feeds on cabbage too. It excretes the [SOURCE: Page_108]

395   glycosinolates in its honeydew, and the impact on parasitoid offspring development is low [SOURCE: Page_108]

396   (Weber et al. 1986, Merritt 1996, Francis et al. 2001). In our study, A. abdominalis has a [SOURCE: Page_108]

397   successful development in M. persicae (0.73 adults in the next generation) compared to no [SOURCE: Page_108]

398   development at all in B. brassicae, indicating that A. abdominalis is strongly affected by [SOURCE: Page_108]

399   glycosinolates. However, A. ervi cannot parasitize M. persicae whereas it is a suitable host [SOURCE: Page_108]

400   (Bruce et al. 2008, Kavallieratos et al. 2004, Colinet et al. 2005), suggesting that the aphid [SOURCE: Page_108]

401   genotype could be involved in this failure (Bilodeau et al. 2013). Aphid ability to sequester [SOURCE: Page_108]

402   the toxic compounds from their plant requires a high specialization of these aphid species [SOURCE: Page_108]

403   (Mooney et al. 2008) and only a few (up to four) parasitoid species can parasite A. nerii [SOURCE: Page_108]

404   and B. brassicae (Kavallieratos et al. 2004); suggesting that robust circumventing [SOURCE: Page_108]

405   mechanisms are needed for a parasitoid to adapt to aphid defense. Furthermore, by [SOURCE: Page_108]

406   contrast with endosymbionts, this is a general aphid defensive against natural enemies in [SOURCE: Page_108]

407   general and not only against parasitoids (Omkar and Mishra 2005, Toft and Wise 1999), [SOURCE: Page_108]

408   general defenses confer a stronger protection against generalist parasitoids compared to [SOURCE: Page_108]

409   the specialized defenses. [SOURCE: Page_108]

410   Finally, host quality could also contribute to a reduction in aphid parasitoid host range (high [SOURCE: Page_108]

411   mortality of the older parasitoid larvae until emergence) or at least modulate their sex ratio [SOURCE: Page_108]

412   (male‐biased) (Thompson 1985, Mackauer 1986, Kouamé and Mackauer 1991, Godfray [SOURCE: Page_108]

413   1994). The host species and the host's age are the two main components of host quality for [SOURCE: Page_108]

101 [SOURCE: Page_108]

414   parasitoid development. However, generalist parasitoids have a weaker choosiness when [SOURCE: Page_109]

415   encountering different hosts as shown in the behavioral results of this study. For example, S. [SOURCE: Page_109]

416   graminum caused a high larval mortality of the three parasitoid species (consistent with [SOURCE: Page_109]

417   Desneux et al. 2009a) and a high value of un‐emerged parasitoids of A. ervi were observed in [SOURCE: Page_109]

418   R. padi despite they are stung mainly by the parasitoids, indicating S. graminum and R. padi [SOURCE: Page_109]

419   to be poor hosts for these parasitoid species. Furthermore, some parasitoids tend to place [SOURCE: Page_109]

420   male eggs in unfavorable hosts (King 1990, Kochetova 1978). For example, the sex ratios of [SOURCE: Page_109]

421   Aphidius colemani emerging adults were more male‐biased from R. padi than from the three [SOURCE: Page_109]

422   other aphid species (M. persicae, S. graminum, and A. gossypii), suggesting that R. padi is a [SOURCE: Page_109]

423   poor‐quality host for A. colemani (Ode et al. 2005). In A. abdominalis, a male‐biased sex [SOURCE: Page_109]

424   ratio was observed in M. dirhodum, M. persicae (green and red), R. padi and S. graminum; [SOURCE: Page_109]

425   but no link among the host plant, the presence of endosymbiont, the color or the host size [SOURCE: Page_109]

426   could explain these results except the low quality of these hosts for A. abdominalis. [SOURCE: Page_109]

427   The preference-performance relationship in generalist parasitoids [SOURCE: Page_109]

428   The meta‐analysis of Cripenberg et al. (2010) of the preference‐performance relationship in [SOURCE: Page_109]

429   phytophagous insects described a relationship between the preference and the [SOURCE: Page_109]

430   performance of specialists and intermediate specialists / generalists and no relationship for [SOURCE: Page_109]

431   generalists and these results are consistent with those obtained in our study, there was no [SOURCE: Page_109]

432   relationship between the preference and the performance of A. abdominalis and D. rapae [SOURCE: Page_109]

433   whereas a strong relationship was observed for A. ervi. The phenotypic plasticity is more [SOURCE: Page_109]

434   critical in generalist parasitoids as well as the transgenerational phenotypic plasticity, and [SOURCE: Page_109]

435   these abilities could explain the lack of the preference‐performance relationship in A. [SOURCE: Page_109]

436   abdominalis and D. rapae, due to their low host selectivity and their high performance [SOURCE: Page_109]

102 [SOURCE: Page_109]

437   among the hosts. A. ervi is considered as an intermediate specialist‐generalist because it [SOURCE: Page_110]

438   showed a successful development only in three of the aphid species stung; and its low [SOURCE: Page_110]

439   selectivity can be explained by its strategy to avoid aphid defenses (highly aggressive and [SOURCE: Page_110]

440   quick oviposition when it encountered hosts; Bilodeau et al. 2013). Furthermore, a possible [SOURCE: Page_110]

441   phylogenetical signal could exist among its hosts (Zepeda‐Paulo et al. 2013); suggesting a [SOURCE: Page_110]

442   host phylogenetic specialization (as for B. communis, Desneux et al. 2012). [SOURCE: Page_110]

443   We demonstrated that the preference‐performance relationship does occur for specialist [SOURCE: Page_110]

444   and intermediate specialist‐generalist parasitoids but not for true generalist ones; likely [SOURCE: Page_110]

445   owing to combined effects of low selectivity and the phenotypic plasticity in generalist [SOURCE: Page_110]

446   parasitoids. The generalists are less affected by specific aphid defenses against them (such [SOURCE: Page_110]

447   as endosymbionts) whereas they are strongly affected by general ones that are used against [SOURCE: Page_110]

448   natural enemies, e.g. aphid ability to sequester the toxic compounds (Desneux et al. 2009a). [SOURCE: Page_110]

449   The preference of generalists is not an accurate proxy of actual parasitoid realized host [SOURCE: Page_110]

450   range, i.e. performance. The occurrence (or lack thereof) of such relationship, as well as the [SOURCE: Page_110]

451   host specificity index, may provide a reliable indicator of actual generalism – specialism in [SOURCE: Page_110]

452   parasitoids. [SOURCE: Page_110]

453 [SOURCE: Page_110]

131 [SOURCE: Page_138]

Chapter 5.         Water stress-mediated bottom-up effects on aphid parasitoid diet breath [SOURCE: Page_139]

Context [SOURCE: Page_139]

In the previous chapter, we investigated the fundamental host ranges of three parasitoids: A. ervi, A. abdominalis and D. rapae under laboratory conditions (Figure 32). However, as seen before, the fundamental host range could differ from the field host range. For example, the field inefficiency of A. abdominalis is well known despite its good parasitism rate in the laboratory (Holler and Haardt 1993). Our next question is to disentangle the difference between fundamental and field host range by defining which environmental factors influence the parasitoid host specificity and how? Within the plant‐aphid – parasitoid tritrophic system, the determinants of the host specificity could come from biotic interactions among them and with other trophic levels, e.g. endosymbionts (see article 1) or from abiotic environmental conditions (Error! Reference source not found.). [SOURCE: Page_139]

132 [SOURCE: Page_139]

Figure 32. Parasitoid responses at each step of parasitism process and possible mechanisms of their mortality (from Desneux 2009a). [SOURCE: Page_140]

Among these factors, plant stress causes the most chronic harmful effects on parasitoid performance in greenhouses compare to the other biotic factors (Error! Reference source not found., Prado et al. 2015). In the field, bottom‐up effects of abiotic factors on plants are significantly stronger than biotic forces (Schuldt et al. 2017). Environmental abiotic stressors, i.e. water supply, salt or nutrition may affect plant quality including, morphology, nutritional quality or defense. Those impacts may reach parasitoids by plant‐mediated or aphid‐ mediated bottom‐up effects (Error! Reference source not found., Hunter 2003). For example, the acceptance, i.e. parasitoid attack and oviposit on) and suitability of an aphid as parasitoid host depends on both aphid host quality (nutritional quality and behavior) and aphid host density (the probability of encounter) (Mackeur et al. 2012). [SOURCE: Page_140]

Figure 33. Synthesis of bottom-up effects of plants on parasitoid community (from Hunter 2003) [SOURCE: Page_140]

We focused on the bottom‐up effect of abiotic stresses on the host specificity of parasitoids. Firstly, we performed an analytic review on the bottom‐up effects of all abiotic stresses on the tri‐trophic plant‐aphid‐parasitoid to define the most relevant factor for our experimental set‐up. Secondly, we chose one parasitoid among our three parasitoids based on previous results (chapter 4). Thirdly, we designed experiments to question the ability of such abiotic factor to modulate the host range of our parasitoid (fig. 32). [SOURCE: Page_140]

133 [SOURCE: Page_140]

1. Choice of a relevant abiotic factor to analyze impacts of bottom-up effect on the host specificity of parasitoids [SOURCE: Page_141]

Gathering data [SOURCE: Page_141]

To gather all articles on the subject 'bottom‐up effects of abiotic stress on aphid parasitoids', we based on the process of meta‐analysis review (Koricheva et al. 2013). The first step of the process consisted of the construction of a database of all relevant articles based on Web of Science ®. [SOURCE: Page_141]

We chose the following combination of keywords for searching on Web of Science ®: (((aphid* OR apheli*) AND parasitoid AND (stress* OR abiotic OR drought OR water OR inundation OR logged OR dry OR dessic* OR sal* OR temperature OR winter OR cold OR heat OR cool OR thermal OR nutri* OR fertili* OR nitrog* OR potassium OR phosphat* OR light OR UV OR ozone OR elevated OR climat* OR weather OR plant condition OR plant quality OR bottom-up OR humidity OR wind)) NOT whitefly NOT moth NOT coccine* NOT ladybeetle). [SOURCE: Page_141]

To eliminate irrelevant studies, we considered only studies that question the bottom‐up effect of abiotic factors on the tritrophic system ‘plant‐aphid – parasitoid'. These studies contain data sets from both optimal and stress conditions (average +/‐ variance). Finally, the following set of criteria were applied: [SOURCE: Page_141]

a.     Research year: all, between 1990 and 2016 b.     Studies on tritrophic: plants – aphids ‐ parasitoids c.     Data: related to the performance of species or population under stress condition versus optimal conditions. d.     Direct impacts of temperature on parasitoids, e.g. storage temperature without plant or aphid presence are not considered. [SOURCE: Page_141]

Based on these keywords, 746 studies were found. By reading titles, abstracts or if necessary, articles, 230 articles that followed our criteria were chosen (annex 1, figure 36). However, only 18 studies provide the required information, i.e. the parameters of three trophic under optimal conditions and abiotic stress; the number of replications for each trophic. Those are studies of Garratt et al. (2010); Bannerman et al. (2011); Johnson et al. (2011); Wu et al. [SOURCE: Page_141]

134 [SOURCE: Page_141]

(2011); Gillespie et al. (2012); Aslam et al. (2013); Cayetano and Vorburger(2013; Ismail et al. (2013); Romo and Tylianakis(2013; Tariq et al. (2013); Le Lann et al. (2014); Meisner et al. (2014); Aqueel et al. (2015); Jerbi‐Elayed et al. (2015); Mace and Mills(2015; Sanders et al. (2015); Flores‐Mejia et al. (2016); Moiroux et al. (2016). [SOURCE: Page_142]

In one the case study of meta‐analysis review (chapter 5, p. 48, Koricheva et al. 2013), initial results were 2845 studies found by keywords, they reduced to 124 relevant papers, and finally, only 20 documents providing necessary information. The process of our data research was hence appropriate. However, more studies should be added to complete the data analysis. [SOURCE: Page_142]

Data extraction and statistical analysis [SOURCE: Page_142]

We extracted all data related to the modifications on different parameters of life‐history traits of the three trophic levels: plant‐aphid‐parasitoids. The parameters for plants were morphology, primary metabolism, and secondary metabolism. The parameters for both aphids and parasitoids were the preference, performance, and population dynamics. The data were extracted from tables or figures using ImageJ. Three experimental scales have been taking on: laboratory, greenhouse or on the field. We quoted the stress type (drought, nutrients, temperature) and the ways those stresses were applied (continuous or pulsed). The data were analyzed by Pearson's Chi‐squared test with Yate continuity correction to compare the performance of plants, aphids, and parasitoids between optimal and stress conditions. The purpose was to find if abiotic factors have a significant impact on the third trophic level, i.e. parasitoids. We counted the number of parameters of performance that reduced (optimal>stress), increased (optimal<stress) or did not change (optimal = stress) under stress conditions. [SOURCE: Page_142]

Results [SOURCE: Page_142]

Abiotic factors have significant impacts on the whole system (Error! Reference source not found., p value < 0.001). Among all types of abiotic stress, continuous water limitation adversely affected parasitoid life‐history traits (p value < 0.001) and plants (p value = 0.013). However, aphids were only marginally impacted (p value = 0.056). There are few data on how [SOURCE: Page_142]

135 [SOURCE: Page_142]

pulsed water stress affected the tri‐trophic system. Huberty and Denno (2004) suggested that pulsed water stress could indirectly benefit aphids as senescence plants release nutrients to the phloem. [SOURCE: Page_143]

The other stressors such as light and temperature imply both bottom‐up effects and direct effects on the other trophic levels (Error! Reference source not found.). Light conditions did not cause significant impacts on plants or aphids (p value > 0.05) but significant impacts on parasitoids (p value = 0.02). Temperatures have negative impacts on all organisms (plants: p value = 0.001; aphids: p value = 0.0098; parasitoids: p value = 0.032). However, it is not clear whether impacts of temperatures on insects are direct or indirect because aphids and parasitoids are temperature sensible (Godfray 1994; van Emden and Harrington 2007). There was no evidence of bottom‐up effects of nutrient stress on the whole system or at each trophic level. [SOURCE: Page_143]

For this study, we focused on water limitation. Firstly, water stress is one of the most common abiotic factors that adversely affected the agriculture, perturbed the agrosystem and cause severe economic and social damage (FAO 2015; Ziolkowska 2016). Secondly, water limitation could have bottom‐up effects (indirect impacts starting from lower trophic level) on parasitoids: however, the incomes are both negative and positive, varying with stress nature and herbivore species (Price 1991; Huberty and Denno 2004; Ramegowda and Senthil‐Kumar 2015). [SOURCE: Page_147]

There are so far six articles relevant to our subject of “bottom‐up effects of water limitation on aphid parasitoids” (table 5). In general, all studies suggest a decrease of parasitoid efficiency in host suppression (Johnson et al. 2011; Shipp et al. 2011; Amini et al. 2012; Tariq et al. 2013; Aslam et al. 2013; Romo and Tylianakis 2013). [SOURCE: Page_147]

Table 3. Articles on the subject: “Impacts of water stress on the tritrophic plant-aphid- parasitoid system.” Results were found from the database of Web of Science ®. Codes of experimental scales: laboratory: L, Greenhouse: G, Field: F, Observation in the field: O. NE stands for all parasitoids. [SOURCE: Page_147]

Year   Scale Parasitoid species Reference [SOURCE: Page_147]

2012       O Diaeretiella rapae     Amini et al. (2012) 2013        L Aphidius ervi         Aslam et al. (2013) 2011        L Aphidius ervi         Johnson et al. (2011) 2013        L Diaeretiella rapae    Romo and Tylianakis (2013) 2011       G Aphelinid spp.         Shipp et al. (2011) 2013        L Aphidius colemani Tariq et al. (2013) [SOURCE: Page_147]

Explanations of possible bottom-up impacts [SOURCE: Page_147]

Effects on parasitoids may be modulated by the herbivorous interactions between aphids and their host plant (Error! Reference source not found.). Water deficiency in plants may decrease the emission of plant volatiles which serves as cues for parasitoids (Weldegergis et al. 2015) resulting in parasitoids less prefer infested plants (Aslam et al. 2013, Tariq et al. 2013). Herbivore density and size could be modulated by the stress tolerance of plants, which [SOURCE: Page_147]

result in affected plant nutritional quality and/or immune system. One scenario of plant stress tolerance is their secondary pathways activated producing insecticidal toxins like phenolic compounds (Caretto et al. 2015). Those toxins to protect infested plants could delay insect growth and make them more susceptible to natural enemies by diminishing the herbivorous immune response or performance (Ibanez et al. 2012). On the contrary, the plant insecticidal compounds may act like “arms race” of aphids by deterring the performance of parasitoids (Züst and Agrawal 2015) if aphids could sequester them. More specifically, aphids can sequester polar toxic compounds or tolerate apolar induced compounds at low concentration (Züst and Agrawal 2016). Morphological modification in plants can influence the overall parasitic efficacy. The structural morphology of plants may be enhanced (Couture et al. 2015) that cause difficulty for herbivores in foraging. The altered plant quality is accompanied by the reduction in aphid population and the loss in a number of survival parasitoids. In another case, the increase in flower‐head size provides shelter for herbivores and reduce the parasitism (Dias et al. 2010). Another critical factor is the presence of symbionts on aphids. Plant host‘s stresses can impact the bacterial population on aphids following by the alter the resistance of aphid to parasitism (Cayetano and Vorburger 2013). Overall, the effects of plant hosts quality under stresses on parasitoids are mutually determined by several factors (Error! Reference source not found.). It thus hard to predict the trend of the host range of parasitoids under bottom‐up effects. [SOURCE: Page_148]

2. Choice of a relevant ‘plant-aphid-parasitoid’ tritrophic system to analyze the host range modulation [SOURCE: Page_148]

Among our three tested parasitoids, we interest in A. ervi. Firstly, A. ervi are intermediate (host) specialist. This intermediate position may allow a shift toward a more generalist or specialist pattern in a variable environment. There is a hypothesis on intermediate specialists/generalists which extinct due to their low efficient adaptation strategy (Colles et al. 2009, Buchi and Vuilleumier (2014). We would like to test the adaptability of A. ervi to modulate its host range under environmental stresses. Moreover, A. ervi is commonly used as a biological model to study ecological concepts and evolution mechanisms (annex chap^5 table 8). These studies may provide hints for mechanisms underlying the results of bottom‐ up effects we observed. [SOURCE: Page_148]

141 [SOURCE: Page_148]

Based on previously established host range part for A. ervi (chap 4, fig. 30), we chose six aphid species assemblage which is different on taxa and biological traits. Three relatively good aphid hosts (more than 40% emerging adults) were selected: Sitobion avenae on wheat and Acyrthosiphon pisum on faba beans and Macrosiphum euphorbiae on potato. Myzus persicae on cabbage were also selected as medium host (10% emerging adults). All of them belong to the Macrosiphini tribe as the host aphid phylogeny was described previously as a significant factor to explain the host range (chap 4). For the other aphid hosts, A. ervi accepted at low ratio and were not capable of forming mummies inside the rest hosts. We excluded them except for Aphis nerii on Asclepias and Rhopalosiphum padi on wheat to also test our hypotheses on bad hosts from the Aphidini tribe. [SOURCE: Page_149]

Figure 34. Host range testing of Aphidius ervi under water limitation condition. The red- boxed aphid names were chosen for the previous experiment (chapter 4): Sitobion avenae and Acyrthosiphon pisum (good hosts), Myzus persicae and Macrosiphum euphorbiae (medium hosts), Rhopalosiphum padi (reported as host in the field), and Aphis nerii (attacked at high rate but unsuitable for A. ervi development). [SOURCE: Page_149]

142 [SOURCE: Page_149]

- Key_Entities: Agriculture, Irrigation

### 3. Experimental set-up: Does water limitation bottom-up effect modulate A. ervi host specificity?

To disentangle the effect of environmental conditions on the host range modulation, we wanted to test the effect of abiotic factors on our host range indexes previously described (chap 4): the host specificity index and the correlation preference‐performance. Our question is whether these indexes would change under water limitation conditions. Firstly, we tested the eventuality of bottom‐up effects on two equally good hosts for A. ervi (article 2), i.e. whether the impact of water limitation could reach and influence the preference and performance of A. ervi. Secondly, we increased the diversity of aphid hosts in quality and quantity to test the impact of bottom‐up effect on the correlation preference‐performance and host specificity. Moreover, if there is an impact, what happens at the lower trophic level that could explain such result (articles 3)? Our hypothesis was that plant water limitation could affect the preference and performance of A. ervi due to modifications at the plant levels such as defense, morphology or nutritional quality as suggested by Becker et al. (2015). As a result, A. ervi host specificity would be impacted by water limitation. [SOURCE: Page_150]

In this study, we chose to apply a long‐term and continuous water stress treatment (30% of soil saturation level) compare to optimal conditions (100% of soil saturation in all plants except 70% in cabbage). To define the relevant level of water, we monitored a pre‐experiment to test the effect of different water conditions on plant and aphid biological traits. The threshold of 30% was a compromise between an apparent effect of water on plant traits compare to optimal conditions and an acceptable rate of aphid survival when reared on plants submitted to water limitation. [SOURCE: Page_150]

We applied the same host range testing method as previous chapter 4. The biological traits of both plants, aphids, and parasitoids were measured to evaluate the impacts of water limitation. We also calculated two host specificity indexes: the Taxonomic index of specificity (Poulin & Mouillot 2005) which put weigh of specificity on the taxonomic distance among hosts; and the Rohde index of specificity (Rohde & Rohde 2008), which measures host specificity based on the parasitism rates on hosts (Chapter 1.4.1). [SOURCE: Page_150]

Water limitation impact preference and performance of good host (article 2) [SOURCE: Page_150]

143 [SOURCE: Page_150]

We were able to show that the impact of water limitation can reach the higher trophic level of natural enemies even when plants and aphids tolerate the applied stress but this effect is not similar in different plant‐aphid complexes, yet the relative host qualities are identical. Plant and aphid traits were negatively affected by water limitation, which might be related to the reduction of host use efficiency of A. ervi. The next question is: whether water limitation impacts on A. ervi performance on sub‐optimal hosts or host feeding on toxic plants? Would the results similar across all plant‐aphid combinations? [SOURCE: Page_151]

Water conditions modulate A. ervi host specificity (article 3) [SOURCE: Page_151]

We observed that water limitation have negative impacts on parasitoid development in medium and good host quality (M. persicae, M. euphorbiae, and S. avenae). The mortality of parasitoids on low‐quality hosts like R. padi or A. nerii remains high which is not different under both water conditions. Our explanation comes from different defense strategies of plants and aphids under combined stresses, and the nutritional quality of both plant and insect. Under severe and/or continuous drought conditions, plants nutrient resources reduce as a trade‐off for stress tolerance which reduces herbivore fitness (Price 1991, Huberty and Denno 2004). At the same time, the hardened structure of plants, the stomatal closure or the increase of plant trichome under stress may cause difficulty for herbivores in foraging (Couture et al. 2015). The two mechanisms probably cause the decrease of A. ervi performance on M. persicae, M. euphorbiae and S. avenae. [SOURCE: Page_151]

However, on native host species (A. pisum), both preference and performance of A. ervi were unchanged. The resistance of A. ervi developed on A. pisum under cascade impacts of drought could be explained by the effectiveness of the placenta‐like structure of larval parasitoid which drives adequate nutrients for larvae, the efficiency of plant symbioses (rhizobacteria) in protecting beans from combined stress. [SOURCE: Page_151]

Besides, we observed that the optimal strategy for A. ervi is not efficient under water limitation i.e. the correlation between preference and performance is lost under stressed conditions compare to optimal conditions. Our study provides evidence for the model of the trade‐off between host range breadth and host use efficiency as suggested by Straub et al. (2011). [SOURCE: Page_151]

144 [SOURCE: Page_151]

Figure 35 Impacts of soil water limitation on the relation preference – performance of Aphidius ervi [SOURCE: Page_152]

Interestingly, the impacts of water limitation on the preference and performance of A. ervi were reflected on the shift of host specificity indexes. Among two indexes, the STD* similar, but the LW SS decreased in compare to OW (0.61 and 0.77, respectively). The result means that A. ervi became more specialist under water limitation as they could develop well on only one aphid host, A. pisum. The decrease of host specificity is relevant to the model of Klinken (1999) (see chapter 1.4). The Rhode index, in that case, is more relevant than the STD* as it could detect the subtle change in host specificity through parasitoid performance and A. ervi, under both LW and OW, had the same host range. [SOURCE: Page_152]

In this study, we highlight the environmental context‐dependent of outcomes of abiotic stress effects. The information of abiotic factors then should be considered before choosing and releasing on BCA on the field. This study also helps to predict the efficiency of one BCA on the field, which also depends on the host range of both herbivores and parasitoids. [SOURCE: Page_152]

145 [SOURCE: Page_152]

Table 4. Biotic and abiotic factors affecting the host specificity of aphid parasitoids (adapted from Hunter 2003, Prado et al. 2015) [SOURCE: Page_153]

1   Article II. Bottom-up effect of water [SOURCE: Page_154]

2   stress on the aphid parasitoid Aphidius [SOURCE: Page_154]

3   ervi (Hymenoptera: Braconidae) [SOURCE: Page_154]

4   Le‐Thu‐Ha Nguyen^1, Lucie Monticelli^1, Nicolas Desneux^1*, Christiane Métay‐Merrien^1, Edwige [SOURCE: Page_154]

5   Amiens‐Desneux^1, Eric Wajnberg^1,2, Anne‐Violette Lavoir^1 [SOURCE: Page_154]

6 [SOURCE: Page_154]

7   1 INRA (French National Institute for Agricultural Research), Université Côte d’Azur, CNRS, [SOURCE: Page_154]

8   UMR 1355‐7254, Institut Sophia Agrobiotech, 06903, Sophia Antipolis, France [SOURCE: Page_154]

9   2 INRIA, Projet Hephaistos, 06902 Sophia Antipolis Cedex, France [SOURCE: Page_154]

10 [SOURCE: Page_154]

11   * Corresponding author: [EMAIL_REDACTED] [SOURCE: Page_154]

147 [SOURCE: Page_154]

12   Abstract: Natural enemies inhabiting agro‐ecosystems such as parasitoids may be primarily [SOURCE: Page_155]

13   affected by stresses inducing bottom‐up effects that in turn modulate their preference (host [SOURCE: Page_155]

14   acceptance) and/or their performance (host suitability). The availability of water is one of [SOURCE: Page_155]

15   the most influential forces that impact plant quality and determines population changes and [SOURCE: Page_155]

16   community structure. In the present study, we have examined the impact of the bottom‐up [SOURCE: Page_155]

17   effect of water stress on host specificity of the aphid parasitoid Aphidius ervi. The cascade [SOURCE: Page_155]

18   impact of abiotic stress on plant hosts varies depending on the aphid‐plant system [SOURCE: Page_155]

19   considered. Water stress applied to beans did not impact the performance of A. ervi on [SOURCE: Page_155]

20   Acyrthosiphum pisum whereas there was a negative impact on Sitobion avenae on wheat. [SOURCE: Page_155]

21   The wheat leaf mass per area was higher on water‐stressed plants than on the well‐watered [SOURCE: Page_155]

22   ones. Various mechanisms, notably related to the production of plant toxins during water [SOURCE: Page_155]

23   stress and host quality, are discussed to explain the bottom‐up effect observed in [SOURCE: Page_155]

24   parasitoids. [SOURCE: Page_155]

25   Keywords: host range, oviposition, tritrophic interactions, abiotic stress, drought [SOURCE: Page_155]

148 [SOURCE: Page_155]

26   Introduction [SOURCE: Page_156]

27   Arthropod communities are influenced by both bottom‐up and top‐down forces in wild and [SOURCE: Page_156]

28   agricultural ecosystems, with bottom‐up forces being characterized by effects occurring [SOURCE: Page_156]

29   from lower trophic levels to higher ones while the top‐down forces show the reverse [SOURCE: Page_156]

30   (Hunter and Price 1992, Johnson 2008). Natural enemies inhabiting agro‐ecosystems such as [SOURCE: Page_156]

31   parasitoids may be largely affected by bottom‐up forces because herbivore hosts represent [SOURCE: Page_156]

32   the entire nutritional and physiological environment during immature parasitoid [SOURCE: Page_156]

33   development, and such herbivores are often strongly impacted by bottom‐up effects (Han et [SOURCE: Page_156]

34   al. 2014, 2016). Parasitoid performance depends mainly on host quality, which in turns is [SOURCE: Page_156]

35   modulated by the host plant and abiotic factors (Vinson, 1990; Godfray, 1994). Also, adult [SOURCE: Page_156]

36   female parasitoid preference, i.e. selection of hosts for laying eggs, is intervened by both [SOURCE: Page_156]

37   their host recognition and acceptance (Godfray 1994). Female choice primarily bases on [SOURCE: Page_156]

38   host‐related, contact, and volatile cues (Vet & Dicke 1992). Therefore, stresses inducing [SOURCE: Page_156]

39   bottom‐up effects on hosts may, in turn, modulates parasitoid preference (host acceptance) [SOURCE: Page_156]

40   and/or their performance (host suitability) as these are critical determinants of the [SOURCE: Page_156]

41   parasitoid host range (Desneux et al. 2009a; 2009b; 2012). For example, plant vigor could [SOURCE: Page_156]

42   modulate dispersal of parasitoids presumably through changes in parasitoid preference [SOURCE: Page_156]

43   when selecting hosts (Kher et al. in 2014) and plant characteristics mediated by growing [SOURCE: Page_156]

44   conditions can impact parasitoid ability to attack host aphids (Desneux and Ramirez‐Romero [SOURCE: Page_156]

45   2009). Availability of critical nutrients to plants, e.g. nitrogen, could affect as well the [SOURCE: Page_156]

46   development of aphid parasitoids (Chesnais et al. in 2016, Aqueel et al. 2015) but effects [SOURCE: Page_156]

47   vary in function of host species considered. All in all, variations in host plant quality may [SOURCE: Page_156]

48   affect directly herbivorous and parasitoid arthropods as well as their relationships. [SOURCE: Page_156]

149 [SOURCE: Page_156]

49          Among abiotic stresses, availability of water is one of primary constraint impacting [SOURCE: Page_157]

50   plant quality; it may trigger changes in multi‐trophic interactions, population dynamics as [SOURCE: Page_157]

51   well as community structure (Hunter and Price 1992, Schoonhoven et al. 2005; Han et al. [SOURCE: Page_157]

52   2015a; 2015b). Plant water content affects its nutritional value which, in turn, has an impact [SOURCE: Page_157]

53   on insect behavior. For example, herbivorous insect choosiness when encountering host [SOURCE: Page_157]

54   plants is influenced by plant quality (Schoonhoven et al. 2005, Metcalfe et al. 2010, [SOURCE: Page_157]

55   Weldegergis et al. 2015), e.g. Mamestra brassicae (moth) preferred to lay their eggs on [SOURCE: Page_157]

56   drought‐stressed plants than on control ones. Furthermore, severe drought decreases the [SOURCE: Page_157]

57   emission of herbivore‐induced plant volatiles that plays a crucial role in host detection by [SOURCE: Page_157]

58   parasitoids and modifies their host selection (Becker et al. 2015). Plant quality has an [SOURCE: Page_157]

59   impact also on the higher trophic level performance through three potential mechanisms: [SOURCE: Page_157]

60   (1) drought stress could promote toxin production by the plant which interferes with the [SOURCE: Page_157]

61   development of parasitoid larvae inside the host, e.g. through sequestration of toxins by the [SOURCE: Page_157]

62   hosts (Malcolm 1989; Ode 2006; Desneux et al. 2009a, (2) hosts could be qualitatively [SOURCE: Page_157]

63   suboptimal in terms of nutritional requirements for parasitoid larvae (Desneux et al. 2009a), [SOURCE: Page_157]

64   and (3) plant toxins may modulate host immunity enabling higher parasitoid performance [SOURCE: Page_157]

65   (Kaplan et al. 2016). [SOURCE: Page_157]

66          Potential bottom‐up effect of drought stress in both preference and performance of [SOURCE: Page_157]

67   parasitoids has scarcely been documented. Such knowledge on the impact of bottom‐up [SOURCE: Page_157]

68   forces on tritrophic interactions plant-herbivore – parasitoid in agroecosystems may help to [SOURCE: Page_157]

69   improve biological control programs (Hunter 2003). To evaluate the effect of water stress on [SOURCE: Page_157]

70   the third trophic level, we studied the preference and performance of Aphidius ervi, an [SOURCE: Page_157]

150 [SOURCE: Page_157]

71   aphid parasitoid, when parasitizing its two aphid hosts Acyrthosiphum pisum and Sitobion [SOURCE: Page_158]

72   avenae when host plants were subjected to drought. [SOURCE: Page_158]

73   Material and Methods [SOURCE: Page_158]

74   Biological materials [SOURCE: Page_158]

75   Two aphid species were tested: Acyrthosiphon pisum on the fava bean (Vicia faba L.) and [SOURCE: Page_158]

76   Sitobion avenae on the common wheat (Triticum aestivum L.). Both colonies were initiated [SOURCE: Page_158]

77   from individuals collected in Brittany, France. They tested negative for secondary bacterial [SOURCE: Page_158]

78   symbionts (Monticelli, Nguyen & Desneux, unpublished data) when screened for main ones [SOURCE: Page_158]

79   known to provide stress adaptability in aphids (Oliver et al. 2012). The endoparasitoid [SOURCE: Page_158]

80   Aphidius ervi (Haliday) was reared on Acyrthosiphon pisum, and both aphid and [SOURCE: Page_158]

81   parasitoid species were reared in a growth chamber at 22 ±2 °C, 65 ± 5 % relative humidity [SOURCE: Page_158]

82   (RH) and 16:8‐h light:dark (L:D). Mummies of Aphidius ervi were collected and kept in the [SOURCE: Page_158]

83   dark at 22°C until adult emergence. New emergent parasitoids were captured and [SOURCE: Page_158]

84   transferred into a 3‐cm diameter cylindrical tube in a 1:1 ratio and were fed with a water [SOURCE: Page_158]

85   solution containing 50% honey. After 24 hours, the mated females were isolated in gelatin [SOURCE: Page_158]

86   capsules before the no‐choice test (see below). [SOURCE: Page_158]

87   Plant water treatment and infestation by aphids [SOURCE: Page_158]

88   Plants were grown from seeds in a climatic chamber (24 ± 1°C, 65 ± 5% RH, 12:12‐h L:D) in [SOURCE: Page_158]

89   plastic pots (9 × 9 × 10 cm) containing organic soil and limestone grains using a 1:1 ratio. A [SOURCE: Page_158]

90   nutrient solution was used to water plants. For a 100‐L stock solution, we added 12.5kg [SOURCE: Page_158]

91   Ca(NO$_3$)2, 0.5kg NH4NO$_3$, 7.5kg KNO$_3$, 3.5kg K2HPO$_4$, 1kg K2SO$_4$, 1.5kg MgSO$_4$, 3L HNO$_3$, 1.5L [SOURCE: Page_158]

151 [SOURCE: Page_158]

92   H3PO$_4$. Micronutrients were provided in the form of 3L Kanieltra 6 Fe (Hydro Azote, [SOURCE: Page_159]

93   Nanterre, France), and iron was supplied by 2.8L of EDTA‐Fe. The stock solution was then [SOURCE: Page_159]

94   diluted with tap water using a ratio of 5L per 100L final volume to obtain the final irrigation [SOURCE: Page_159]

95   solution. One week after sowing, bean and wheat seedlings reaching four to eight [SOURCE: Page_159]

96   centimeters in height, were transferred under laboratory conditions (23 ± 2 °C, 65 ± 5% RH [SOURCE: Page_159]

97   and 16:8‐h L:D). One‐week‐old bean and wheat plants were randomly assigned two water [SOURCE: Page_159]

98   levels: 30% and 100% water volumes (referred to as water stress and control conditions). [SOURCE: Page_159]

99   Both stressed and control plants received the same amount of the necessary nutrient [SOURCE: Page_159]

100   solution for the highest level stressed plants; Control plants then received tap water to [SOURCE: Page_159]

101   reach their irrigation levels. The purpose was to maintain the same amount of nutrients and [SOURCE: Page_159]

102   only to vary the volume of water each plant received during the experiments. [SOURCE: Page_159]

103          Three weeks after the beginning of the water treatment, wheat and bean plants [SOURCE: Page_159]

104   were offered to aphid colonies in well aerated 60x^60x^60cm mesh cages. New experimental [SOURCE: Page_159]

105   colonies were initiated by introducing fourth‐instar aphids into the control and water stress [SOURCE: Page_159]

106   plant cages until new aphid generations appeared. Aphid parents were then removed. Aphid [SOURCE: Page_159]

107   colonies were maintained from five to ten generations until the experiments ended. New 3‐ [SOURCE: Page_159]

108   week treated wheat or beans were added each week and stayed from one to two weeks in [SOURCE: Page_159]

109   the cages before being replaced. The aphids were used for no‐choice host specificity testing [SOURCE: Page_159]

110   as described by Desneux et al. (2009a). [SOURCE: Page_159]

111   Plant traits: height and leaf mass area [SOURCE: Page_159]

112   Plant height and leaf mass area (LMA) were recorded before infestation. The LMA (g.m‐2) is [SOURCE: Page_159]

113   calculated as the dry leaf mass divided by the leaf area. To calculate the leaf area, we cut off [SOURCE: Page_159]

152 [SOURCE: Page_159]

114   leaves, flattened them between transparent plastic sheets, scanned (Sharp MX‐3140 [SOURCE: Page_160]

115   scanner) and measured with the ImageJ freeware. The leaves were then put in an oven for [SOURCE: Page_160]

116   24 h at 70°C and were weighted. [SOURCE: Page_160]

117   Experiment 1: Host acceptability and preference [SOURCE: Page_160]

118   We used a no‐choice bioassay (adapted from Desneux et al. 2009, 2012) to study parasitoid [SOURCE: Page_160]

119   female host selection behavior, i.e. preference). Female preference was divided into three [SOURCE: Page_160]

120   action steps, from the first contact with the host to the actual stinging of the host. These [SOURCE: Page_160]

121   actions were defined to categorize female parasitoid behaviors when encountering tested [SOURCE: Page_160]

122   hosts : (1) detection – when the parasitoid touched the aphid with its antenna; (2) [SOURCE: Page_160]

123   acceptance – when the female bent its abdomen and attacked aphid hosts (3) successful [SOURCE: Page_160]

124   stinging – when the female inserted its ovipositor inside the aphid and withdrew it (often [SOURCE: Page_160]

125   resulting in aphid movement). To observe parasitoid behavior, we placed one aphid, and [SOURCE: Page_160]

126   one female parasitoid in a 3‐cm diameter and 1‐cm high dome. They were both naive and [SOURCE: Page_160]

127   had never been in contact with their counterparts to avoid learning‐by‐experience impacts. [SOURCE: Page_160]

128   All insect behaviors were noted for 5 minutes or until successful stinging had taken place. [SOURCE: Page_160]

129   The standard aphid size was chosen based on the size of the 2nd Acyrthosiphon pisum instar, [SOURCE: Page_160]

130   known as the preferred instar host of Aphidius ervi (Sequeira and Mackauer 1994). 30‐36 [SOURCE: Page_160]

131   replicates were performed for each aphid species and each watering conditions in [SOURCE: Page_160]

132   experiment 1. [SOURCE: Page_160]

133   Experiment 2: Host suitability and performance [SOURCE: Page_160]

134   Innate parasitoid performance is determined by the survival of the immature parasitoid [SOURCE: Page_160]

135   stages (egg, larvae, and pupae) inside aphid hosts. To follow up stung aphid development, [SOURCE: Page_160]

153 [SOURCE: Page_160]

136   the aphids previously stung during Experiment 1 (see above) were reared individually in clip [SOURCE: Page_161]

137   cage system on leaflets of water stress or control plants (according to the sources of host [SOURCE: Page_161]

138   aphids tested) for 15 days or until mummification. The clip cages made from 100‐μm‐pore‐ [SOURCE: Page_161]

139   size Corning® cell strainer (Corning, Inc) were adapted from standard clip‐cages (e.g. [SOURCE: Page_161]

140   Mouttet et al. 2011). Additional replicates were carried out without behavioral observations [SOURCE: Page_161]

141   to obtain sufficient replicates for the follow‐up survival test. The number of mummies, [SOURCE: Page_161]

142   adults and the sex ratio of parasitoids was recorded. 51‐89 replicates were performed for [SOURCE: Page_161]

143   each aphid species and each watering conditions in experiment 2. [SOURCE: Page_161]

144   Statistical analyses [SOURCE: Page_161]

145   All statistical analyses were performed using R.3.3.3 software (R Core Team, Vienna, Austria [SOURCE: Page_161]

146   2009). Effect of water treatment on plant height and plant leaf mass area was tested using [SOURCE: Page_161]

147   ANOVA. The proportion of aphids detected, accepted and successfully stung were compared [SOURCE: Page_161]

148   per aphid species using pairwise Fisher tests, with Bonferroni adjustment for multiple [SOURCE: Page_161]

149   comparisons. The proportion of each parasitoid developmental stage and the sex ratio was [SOURCE: Page_161]

150   also analyzed per aphid species using pairwise Fisher tests, with Bonferroni adjustment for [SOURCE: Page_161]

151   multiple comparisons. [SOURCE: Page_161]

152   Results [SOURCE: Page_161]

153   Impact of water stress on plant traits: height and leaf mass area (LMA). [SOURCE: Page_161]

154   Bean and wheat heights were significantly impacted by water stress treatment. Bean height [SOURCE: Page_161]

155   was severely reduced by 27.5% (37.47cm ± 1.53 vs. 27.16cm ±0.88) (F$_1$,18 = 35.61, P < 0.01) [SOURCE: Page_161]

156   and wheat height was decreased by 9% (35cm ± 1.48 vs. 32cm ± 0.78) (F$_1$,26 = 5.8, P = 0.02) [SOURCE: Page_161]

154 [SOURCE: Page_161]

157   (Figure 1, A). Moreover, the wheat LMA was reduced by 15% in comparison to control [SOURCE: Page_162]

158   samples (F$_1$,23 = 7.2, P = 0.013) (Figure 1, B). However, there was no evidence of impact of [SOURCE: Page_162]

159   water stress on the bean LMA (F$_1$,54 = 0.5, P = 0.825). [SOURCE: Page_162]

160   Impact of water stress on A. ervi preference and performance [SOURCE: Page_162]

161   Experiment 1: Preference - Detection, acceptance and successful sting rates of A. ervi on [SOURCE: Page_162]

162   aphids A. pisum and S. avenae were not impacted by the water stress treatment (Fisher [SOURCE: Page_162]

163   exact test: P > 0.05) (Table 1). The proportions of A. pisum detected, accepted or stung by A. [SOURCE: Page_162]

164   ervi were always higher than 0.9 regardless of water treatment. The proportions of S. [SOURCE: Page_162]

165   avenae detected, accepted or stung by A. ervi were consistently higher than 0.80 regardless [SOURCE: Page_162]

166   of water treatment. [SOURCE: Page_162]

167   Experiment 2: Performance - When A. ervi parasitized A. pisum, the number of eggs and [SOURCE: Page_162]

168   larvae found during dissections, as well as mummies produced and adults emerging did not [SOURCE: Page_162]

169   change significantly in function of water stress (Fisher exact test: all P > 0.05) (Figure 2A). [SOURCE: Page_162]

170   When Aphidius ervi encountered S. avenae on well‐watered plants, there was no parasitoid [SOURCE: Page_162]

171   mortality (Fisher exact test: all P > 0.05) (Figure 2B). By contrast, there was parasitoid [SOURCE: Page_162]

172   mortality at the mummy stage as adult emergence significantly reduced under water stress, [SOURCE: Page_162]

173   from 0.63 aphids containing a parasitoid egg to 0.29 adults emerging (Fisher exact test: P < [SOURCE: Page_162]

174   0.01). The sex ratio of parasitoid adults emerging from A. pisum (control: 0.88, water stress: [SOURCE: Page_162]

175   0.74) or S. avenae (control: 0.72, water stress: 0.79) did not differ as function of water [SOURCE: Page_162]

176   treatments (P = 0.22 and 0.35, respectively). [SOURCE: Page_162]

177   Discussion [SOURCE: Page_162]

155 [SOURCE: Page_162]

178   The bottom‐up effect of water stress on the aphid parasitoid Aphidius ervi was assessed [SOURCE: Page_163]

179   when encountering two different aphid species on two host plant species. The experimental [SOURCE: Page_163]

180   design efficiently enabled the water stress to affect the plants; plant height was decreased [SOURCE: Page_163]

181   for both plants, as well as leaf mass area in the case of wheat. The cascade impact of this [SOURCE: Page_163]

182   abiotic stress, through host plant of aphid hosts, varied depending on the aphid‐plant [SOURCE: Page_163]

183   system considered. Successful parasitoid offspring development reduced when A. ervi was [SOURCE: Page_163]

184   tested on S. avenae reared on water‐stressed wheat vs. well water wheat plant. By contrast, [SOURCE: Page_163]

185   no impact of such abiotic stress was detected when comparing offspring development of A. [SOURCE: Page_163]

186   ervi on A. pisum on stressed vs. well‐watered bean plants. Also, there was no bottom‐up [SOURCE: Page_163]

187   effect on parasitoid preference (behaviors) when parasitoid encountered the two aphids on [SOURCE: Page_163]

188   water‐stressed plants vs. well‐watered ones; parasitoid females did not adjust their [SOURCE: Page_163]

189   behavior according to potential bottom‐up effect occurring in the host‐plant systems they [SOURCE: Page_163]

190   were subjected. [SOURCE: Page_163]

191          Typical parasitic behaviors of pro‐ovigenic parasitoids A. ervi, i.e. short lifespan and [SOURCE: Page_163]

192   initial large egg load at adult emergence such as A. ervi (Dieckhoff et al. 2014) may explain, [SOURCE: Page_163]

193   to some extent, the low choosiness of parasitoid female when encountering aphids on [SOURCE: Page_163]

194   water‐stressed plants. Pro‐ovigenic parasitoids tend to accept stinging in most encountered [SOURCE: Page_163]

195   host species belonging to the tribe of their preferred host (Desneux et al. 2009a; 2009c; [SOURCE: Page_163]

196   Monticelli et al. 2018). Water stress applied to bean plant did not impact parasitoid [SOURCE: Page_163]

197   offspring performance on Acyrthosiphum pisum. Therefore the parasitoid females did not [SOURCE: Page_163]

198   need to lower their oviposition rate when encountering hosts despite that these hosts were [SOURCE: Page_163]

199   feeding on plants under suboptimal growth conditions (e.g. see Figure 1). By contrast, [SOURCE: Page_163]

200   offspring development was affected when Aphidius ervi parasitized S. avenae on water‐ [SOURCE: Page_163]

156 [SOURCE: Page_163]

201   stressed wheat plants (decreased survival at pupae stage), but parasitoid females did not [SOURCE: Page_164]

202   lower their oviposition on these potentially suboptimal hosts. It remains unclear whether [SOURCE: Page_164]

203   parasitoid females were not able to identify S. avenae on water‐stressed wheat as [SOURCE: Page_164]

204   suboptimal hosts or if female parasitoids recognized them as suboptimal ones but decided [SOURCE: Page_164]

205   to sting these hosts anyway. A. ervi is an intermediate specialist (Monticelli et al. 2018) and [SOURCE: Page_164]

206   is partially able to avoid suboptimal hosts. The lower rate of eggs recovered during [SOURCE: Page_164]

207   dissections in stung S. avenae on water‐stressed wheat vs. on those on regular wheat plants [SOURCE: Page_164]

208   may hint that A. ervi females did identify the suboptimal hosts and adjusted their [SOURCE: Page_164]

209   selectiveness partially. [SOURCE: Page_164]

210          Few studies have evaluated the impact of aphid nutrition status on immature [SOURCE: Page_164]

211   parasitoid development and field observations suggested lower parasitoid performance, [SOURCE: Page_164]

212   notably reduced offspring production, of aphids and parasitoid populations under [SOURCE: Page_164]

213   drought stress (Aslam et al. 2013, Romo and Tylianakis 2013). Three potential mechanisms [SOURCE: Page_164]

214   may explain bottom‐up effects of water stress on parasitoid performance (Kaplan et al. [SOURCE: Page_164]

215   2016): (i) increased sequestration of plant toxins in hosts, owing to a shift in resource [SOURCE: Page_164]

216   allocation from primary to secondary metabolism in water‐stressed plants (Mewis et al. [SOURCE: Page_164]

217   2012); (ii) reduced resource availability for parasitoid offspring development; such effect [SOURCE: Page_164]

218   may cascade from the first mechanism as primary plant metabolism reduced in stressed [SOURCE: Page_164]

219   plants, and it may cascade to lower resources for aphids, and (iii) modulation of host [SOURCE: Page_164]

220   immunity owing to presence of plant toxins. [SOURCE: Page_164]

221          The two first mechanisms may explain, in part, the lower performance of A. ervi on S. [SOURCE: Page_164]

222   avenae when developing on water‐stressed wheat plants. Both Acyrthosiphum pisum and S. [SOURCE: Page_164]

223   avenae use general and specific proteins that interfere with plant defensive pathways [SOURCE: Page_164]

157 [SOURCE: Page_164]

224   during feeding (Urbanska et al. 1998, Rao et al. 2013). However, A. pisum produces [SOURCE: Page_165]

225   metalloproteases in saliva, and this does not occur in the case of S. avenae (Carolan et al. [SOURCE: Page_165]

226   2009, Will et al. 2013). These proteases break down plant phloem proteins into smaller [SOURCE: Page_165]

227   nitrogen compounds used for both modulation‐suppression of plant defensive hormones [SOURCE: Page_165]

228   and plant feeding (Furch et al. 2014, Furch et al. 2015); they may lower potential adverse [SOURCE: Page_165]

229   bottom effects toward parasitoid developing in A. pisum on water‐stressed plants. It might [SOURCE: Page_165]

230   contribute to A. pisum resistance to plant nitrogen depletion under drought conditions and [SOURCE: Page_165]

231   may have enabled Aphidius ervi to develop well in this host whereas parasitoid offspring [SOURCE: Page_165]

232   development in S. avenae may have been impaired owing to accumulated secondary [SOURCE: Page_165]

233   metabolites and/or reduced proteins availability in this aphid. Such observation still may [SOURCE: Page_165]

234   have to be confirmed by measures of toxins and/proteins content in S. avenae developing [SOURCE: Page_165]

235   on water‐stressed plants vs. wheat cropped in well‐watered conditions. [SOURCE: Page_165]

236          Reduced aphid size, as well as general resource availability in host aphids, could also [SOURCE: Page_165]

237   be a factor underneath suboptimal development of parasitoid offspring in S. avenae. There [SOURCE: Page_165]

238   is a positive relationship between plant vigor and herbivore weight in phytophagous [SOURCE: Page_165]

239   arthropods as well as between their weight and potential parasitoid size (Teder and [SOURCE: Page_165]

240   Tummaru 2002). Parasitoids could fail to develop in small hosts owing to low‐poor resource [SOURCE: Page_165]

241   availability (Godfray 1994). Sitobion avenae size may be caused by poor plant quality and [SOURCE: Page_165]

242   could be responsible for the reduction of A. ervi parasitism rate under drought stress. [SOURCE: Page_165]

243   However, aphid length data are lacking to validate this hypothesis in our study. Also, host [SOURCE: Page_165]

244   size may impact parasitoid fitness by modulating sex ratio, i.e. inducing a male‐biased sex [SOURCE: Page_165]

245   ratio; parasitoid females lay preferentially more female eggs in large hosts and males in [SOURCE: Page_165]

246   small ones (Godfray 1994). Sex ratio did not vary when A. ervi parasitized the two aphid [SOURCE: Page_165]

158 [SOURCE: Page_165]

247   species on well‐watered plants and water‐stressed ones, suggesting that A. ervi did not [SOURCE: Page_166]

248   discern or consider the variability in host quality. All in all, a shortage of resources needed to [SOURCE: Page_166]

249   complete parasitoid development in aphid is thought to affect parasitoid offspring mostly at [SOURCE: Page_166]

250   the pupae stage, notably at the time the larvae produce silk for mummy cocoon (Desneux et [SOURCE: Page_166]

251   al. 2009a) whereas sequestration of toxins by aphids proved to be lethal mostly for younger [SOURCE: Page_166]

252   offspring (from egg to larvae or from larvae to pupae). Therefore, reduced development of [SOURCE: Page_166]

253   A. ervi in S. avenae may be more likely linked to diminished host food quality and/or [SOURCE: Page_166]

254   quantity rather than sequestration of toxins by this aphid on the water‐stressed wheat [SOURCE: Page_166]

255   plant. [SOURCE: Page_166]

256            LMA is an important trait in predicting plant adaptability strategies and/or mortality [SOURCE: Page_166]

257   pattern under changing environments (Poorter et al. 2009, Anderegg et al. 2016). Wheat [SOURCE: Page_166]

258   plants under stress had a lower height, and higher LMA than well‐watered plants (e.g. Blum [SOURCE: Page_166]

259   and Sullivan 1997) and changes in leaf chemical composition mediated by LMA might reflect [SOURCE: Page_166]

260   the change(s) occurring in the vascular system on which aphids fed (Auclair 1963, Will et al. [SOURCE: Page_166]

261   2013). LMA in herbaceous plant correlates positively to leaf carbon concentration but [SOURCE: Page_166]

262   negatively to leaf nitrogen concentration, organic acids and protein concentration (Poorter [SOURCE: Page_166]

263   et al. 2009, De La Riva et al. 2016). Plants with higher LMA are associated with lower N [SOURCE: Page_166]

264   content, and it may lower aphid feeding efficiency and subsequently its quality as host for [SOURCE: Page_166]

265   parasitoid. By contrast, bean height was lower, and its LMA did not alter under the water [SOURCE: Page_166]

266   stress. Beans tend to avoid drought by reducing evaporation surface and leaf stomatal [SOURCE: Page_166]

267   conductance which is the most significant morphological adaptation in this species (Lizana [SOURCE: Page_166]

268   et al. 2006, Lonbani and Arzani 2011, Beebe et al. 2013). Beans did not show increased LMA, [SOURCE: Page_166]

269   suggesting a lower impact of water stress on plant physiology in bean; it may explain the [SOURCE: Page_166]

159 [SOURCE: Page_166]

270   lack of bottom‐up effect on A. ervi parasitism when encountering Acyrthosiphum pisum on [SOURCE: Page_167]

271   water‐stressed host plants. However, the use of the LMA trait for drought adaptability is [SOURCE: Page_167]

272   insufficient and controversial because of plant diversity and the nature of the stress [SOURCE: Page_167]

273   (Marechaux et al. 2015, He and Dijkstra 2014). Further research will require more plant [SOURCE: Page_167]

274   physiological traits to be measured, e.g. photosynthesis and C/N ratio, to deepen our [SOURCE: Page_167]

275   understanding of mechanisms underlying plant stress survival and potential cascading [SOURCE: Page_167]

276   effects toward second and third trophic levels. [SOURCE: Page_167]

277   The present study stressed the importance of abiotic conditions in plant‐herbivore‐ [SOURCE: Page_167]

278   parasitoid interactions and notably host use by parasitoids. Bottom‐up effects may cascade [SOURCE: Page_167]

279   to third trophic level and modulate performance of parasitoids when parasitizing their [SOURCE: Page_167]

280   hosts. Such effects may have a broad impact on insect communities, e.g. it may affect diet [SOURCE: Page_167]

281   breath of parasitoids through narrowing their host range making suitable hosts to turn into [SOURCE: Page_167]

282   poorly suitable or unsuitable ones. [SOURCE: Page_167]

## 13   ABSTRACT

14   Shift in the ecological specialization of herbivorous and predatory species may occur [SOURCE: Page_183]

15   through the indirect impact of abiotic stresses arising at lower trophic levels, e.g. in plants [SOURCE: Page_183]

16   (effects so‐called bottom‐up effects). A tritrophic system "plant‐aphid (sap‐feeding [SOURCE: Page_183]

17   herbivores) – parasitoid (Aphidius ervi, Hymenoptera: Braconidae: Aphidiinae)” was used as [SOURCE: Page_183]

18   a biological model to study such potential shift in parasitoid host specificity owing to [SOURCE: Page_183]

19   bottom‐up impact; we measured possible effects on both behavioral and physiological [SOURCE: Page_183]

20   parasitoid traits. It was investigated using six host plant‐aphid complexes, and water [SOURCE: Page_183]

21   limitation were used as abiotic stress inducing the bottom‐up effect. Also, the impact of [SOURCE: Page_183]

22   water stress on host plants and aphid life‐history traits were assessed. We demonstrated [SOURCE: Page_183]

23   that bottom‐up effects effectively occurred on parasitoid host specificity and that it can [SOURCE: Page_183]

24   modulate its actual host range. The parasitoid choosiness was not drastically affected by [SOURCE: Page_183]

25   bottom‐up effect while aphid host suitability for parasitoid offspring development was [SOURCE: Page_183]

26   decreased in three out of the six aphid species tested. Besides, water stress negatively [SOURCE: Page_183]

27   affected plant primary metabolism‐related mechanisms, e.g. leaf transpiration rate and 28   stomatal conductance, and it cascaded to aphids; their development speed and growth [SOURCE: Page_183]

29   were decreased for half of aphid species considered. Adverse bottom‐up effects observed 30   on aphid hosts were linked to subsequent parasitoid performance on these hosts; except for [SOURCE: Page_183]

31   one aphid species, those showing reduced survival, generation time and intrinsic rate of [SOURCE: Page_183]

32   population increase (rm) proved to be suboptimal hosts for the parasitoid when plants were [SOURCE: Page_183]

33   suffering water stress. This research highlights the potential impact of abiotic‐related stress [SOURCE: Page_183]

34   on actual host specificity in parasitoids. [SOURCE: Page_183]

35   Keywords: host specificity, preference‐performance hypothesis, tritrophic interactions, [SOURCE: Page_183]

36   water limitation, biological control, biological control agents [SOURCE: Page_183]

37 [SOURCE: Page_183]

38 [SOURCE: Page_183]

176 [SOURCE: Page_183]
