## 39   INTRODUCTION

40   Patterns of resources utilization of one species, or ecological specialization, are determined [SOURCE: Page_184]

41   by its functional traits during interactions with other organisms and abiotic factors in an [SOURCE: Page_184]

42   ecosystem. Shifts in ecological specialization may be caused by abiotic stresses occurring [SOURCE: Page_184]

43   from lower trophic levels and reaching the top through direct and indirect interactions [SOURCE: Page_184]

44   among organisms. The understanding of such bottom‐up effects of abiotic stresses is [SOURCE: Page_184]

45   essential to predict one organism responses under fluctuating environmental conditions [SOURCE: Page_184]

46   (Wiens 2016); the community shift direction and quantitative impacts of community shift on [SOURCE: Page_184]

47   the wild and agricultural ecosystems (Devictor et al. 2010; Moe et al. 2013). The tri‐trophic [SOURCE: Page_184]

48   plant‐aphid‐parasitoid system is a good biological model in both ecology and application [SOURCE: Page_184]

49   because of the biodiversity of insects in the terrestrial ecosystem, the tight co‐evolution of [SOURCE: Page_184]

50   organisms within the complex and the importance of parasitoid in biological OW as natural [SOURCE: Page_184]

51   enemies against aphids (Loxdale et al. 2011). The host specificity of a parasitoid is its ability [SOURCE: Page_184]

52   to exploit aphid hosts. It is shaped by parasitoid preference (host acceptance) and parasitoid [SOURCE: Page_184]

53   performance (host suitability) which are influenced by aphid quality, aphid food source, [SOURCE: Page_184]

54   plant quality and interactions within this system (Desneux et al. 2009; Desneux et al. 2012; [SOURCE: Page_184]

55   Godfray 1994; Klinken 1999; Poulin and Mouillot 2005). The diet of aphid hosts can affect [SOURCE: Page_184]

56   host suitability for immature aphid parasitoid, however, evidences are scarce. Soil nitrogen [SOURCE: Page_184]

57   fertilizer could improve parasitoid fitness at moderate quantity (Aqueel et al. 2015). [SOURCE: Page_184]

58          Water stress is among most severe abiotic stresses in current decades. It affects 45% [SOURCE: Page_184]

59   of global farming lands, is predicted to increase at both intensity and frequency (Long and [SOURCE: Page_184]

60   Ort 2010), causes nearly 20% agriculture damage (FAO 2015) and biodiversity loss (Archaux [SOURCE: Page_184]

61   and Wolters 2006). Field observations of aphid and their parasitoid populations under water [SOURCE: Page_184]

177 [SOURCE: Page_184]

62   stress suggest a depletion in parasitoid performance (Aslam et al. 2013; Romo and [SOURCE: Page_185]

63   Tylianakis 2013). The water availability mediated by plants triggers a change in population [SOURCE: Page_185]

64   and community structure at the top level (Han et al. 2015a; Han et al. 2015b; Hunter and [SOURCE: Page_185]

65   Price 1992). The outcomes of stress‐based modifications at natural enemy level may be [SOURCE: Page_185]

66   predicted by variations in plants which directly affect natural enemies or mediate the [SOURCE: Page_185]

67   variations in aphid quality as hosts, e.g. through reduction of plant volatiles such as [SOURCE: Page_185]

68   synomones under water deficiency (Aslam et al. 2013; Dias et al. 2010; Tariq et al. 2013; [SOURCE: Page_185]

69   Weldegergis et al. 2015). Impacts of abiotic stress on parasitoid may also be mediated by [SOURCE: Page_185]

70   aphids such as modifications in aphid availability (density), nutritional quality (size and [SOURCE: Page_185]

71   content), defenses (by morphology, immune system or symbionts), and growth rate, i.e. [SOURCE: Page_185]

72   lower host density) because of plant morphological, physiological and chemical [SOURCE: Page_185]

73   modifications (Hunter 2003). The overall effects may be either negative or positive on aphid [SOURCE: Page_185]

74   performance but often remain unclear for parasitoids. Several studies reported no impact of [SOURCE: Page_185]

75   fruit fly (Martinez‐Ramirez et al. 2016) and leaf miner (Dong et al. 2018) parasitoids. On the [SOURCE: Page_185]

76   negative side, plant biomass loss and plant poor nutrient contents relate to the reduction of [SOURCE: Page_185]

77   aphid population and performance (Johnson et al. 2011; McVean and Dixon 2001; Mewis et [SOURCE: Page_185]

78   al. 2012; Simpson et al. 2012). Plant trichome increase contributes to plant resistance [SOURCE: Page_185]

79   against herbivores (Glas et al. 2012; Handley et al. 2005). In the positive side, stomatal plant [SOURCE: Page_185]

80   closure improves aphid feeding under elevated CO$_2$ (Sun et al. 2015). The plant traits [SOURCE: Page_185]

81   variations are determined by plant defense strategies under a combination of stresses and [SOURCE: Page_185]

82   their nature such as intensity and frequency. Under abiotic stress and aphid infestations, [SOURCE: Page_185]

83   plants can produce both more or less defensive allelochemicals. For example, glucosinate [SOURCE: Page_185]

84   concentration in cabbage and cardenolide in milkweed are reduced under water stress, [SOURCE: Page_185]

85   respectively (Khan et al. 2010; Mewis et al. 2012). By contrast, the secondary pathways in [SOURCE: Page_185]

178 [SOURCE: Page_185]

86   plants could also be activated under stress to produce more insecticidal toxins, e.g. phenolic [SOURCE: Page_186]

87   compounds (Caretto et al. 2015). Those toxins could delay the growth of phytophagous [SOURCE: Page_186]

88   insects as well as making them more susceptible to natural enemies (Ibanez et al. 2012). [SOURCE: Page_186]

89   Bottom‐up effects may also impair endosymbionts harbored by aphids, thus possibly making [SOURCE: Page_186]

90   them more vulnerable to parasitism (Cayetano and Vorburger 2013). However, these [SOURCE: Page_186]

91   allelochemicals can even be sequestered by specialist herbivores and be used as protection [SOURCE: Page_186]

92   against parasitism and/or predation (Züst and Agrawal 2015). So far, previous studies hinted [SOURCE: Page_186]

93   that bottom‐up effects on host‐parasitoid relationships could not be readily generalized and [SOURCE: Page_186]

94   inferred from plant responses to a specific individual stress (Suzuki et al. 2014); the studies [SOURCE: Page_186]

95   assessing potential bottom‐up effects should be carried out using a range of tri‐trophic [SOURCE: Page_186]

96   complexes under well‐defined stress conditions to enable providing broad significance. [SOURCE: Page_186]

97          In the present study, we studied the impact of a bottom‐up force, the water stress, [SOURCE: Page_186]

98   on critical determinants of host specificity (Desneux et al. 2009) of the specialist‐generalist [SOURCE: Page_186]

99   aphid parasitoid Aphidius ervi. Based on a previous study on A. ervi diet breath (Monticelli [SOURCE: Page_186]

100   et al. 2018), we selected six aphid – host plant complexes to assess the impact of water [SOURCE: Page_186]

101   stress on the parasitoid host specificity: Acyrthosiphon pisum ‐ bean, Sitobion avenae – [SOURCE: Page_186]

102   wheat, Rhopalosipum padi ‐ wheat, Myzus persicae ‐ cabbage, Aphis nerii ‐ milkweed, and [SOURCE: Page_186]

103   Macrosiphum euphorbiae ‐ potato. They were chosen as meeting one the following criteria [SOURCE: Page_186]

104   in the absence of water stress (Monticelli et al. 2018): (i) high quality host, i.e. high [SOURCE: Page_186]

105   parasitoid offspring survival until adulthood, or (ii) suboptimal host, i.e. average mortality [SOURCE: Page_186]

106   occurring before adult emergence. These two conditions should enable detecting possible [SOURCE: Page_186]

107   adverse effects (case i) and positive effects (case ii) on parasitoid offspring development, [SOURCE: Page_186]

108   notably owing potential modifications in plant biochemistry and/or host aphid physiology [SOURCE: Page_186]

179 [SOURCE: Page_186]

109   (which is known to impair parasitoid development at larva stage, Desneux et al. 2009). Also, [SOURCE: Page_187]

110   life‐history traits of aphids and plants were recorded to further depict results on parasitoid [SOURCE: Page_187]

111   host specificity under bottom‐up force conditions. The central hypothesis was tested: (i) [SOURCE: Page_187]

112   parasitoid females may show lower preference toward hosts under bottom‐up force owing [SOURCE: Page_187]

113   to decreased quantity and/or quality of chemical cues from host aphids and/or plants, (ii) [SOURCE: Page_187]

114   parasitoid performance would increase on generalist aphids fed on toxic plants and [SOURCE: Page_187]

115   decrease on aphids fed on non‐toxic plants, (iii) the relationship preference‐performance [SOURCE: Page_187]

116   could change under water stress impacts. [SOURCE: Page_187]

## 117   MATERIELS AND METHODS

118   Biological system [SOURCE: Page_187]

119   The parasitoid Aphidius ervi (Haliday) was reared on Acyrthosiphon pisum for 20 generations [SOURCE: Page_187]

120   in a growth chamber (23 ±2 °C, 65 ± 5 % RH, 16:8‐h L: D). The host specificity of A. ervi was [SOURCE: Page_187]

121   tested in six aphid‐plant complexes under two water regimes: optimal water supply (OW) [SOURCE: Page_187]

122   and limited water supply (LW), the irrigation volumes were 100% and 30% of saturated soil [SOURCE: Page_187]

123   volume for five species except for Myzus persicae 70% and 30% of saturated soil volume [SOURCE: Page_187]

124   was applies. The six complexes were: Acyrthosiphon pisum (Harris) on fava bean, Vicia faba [SOURCE: Page_187]

125   L. (referred as Ap^100 and Ap^30 for OW and LW); Sitobion avenae (Fabricius) and [SOURCE: Page_187]

126   Rhopalosiphum padi (Linnaeus) on common wheat, Triticum aestivum L. (Sa^100 and Sa^30, R. [SOURCE: Page_187]

127   padi^100 and R. padi^30, respectively); Myzus persicae (Sulzer) on cabbage, Brassica oleracea [SOURCE: Page_187]

128   var. capitate (M. persicae^70 and M. persicae^30); Aphis nerii (Boyer de Fonscolombe) on [SOURCE: Page_187]

129   milkweed, Asclepias incarnata (An^100 and An^30) and Macrosiphum euphorbiae (Thomas ) [SOURCE: Page_187]

130   on potato, Solanum tuberosum (Me^100 and Me^30). [SOURCE: Page_187]

180 [SOURCE: Page_187]

131   Impacts of water stress on plants [SOURCE: Page_188]

132   Plant water stress treatment – The water stress treatment was applied from the previous [SOURCE: Page_188]

133   study of Nguyen, LTH, et al., (2018, in prep). The experimental plants were grown from [SOURCE: Page_188]

134   seeds in a climatic chamber (23 ±2 °C, 65 ± 5 % RH, 12:12‐h L:D) in plastic pots (9 × 9 × 10 [SOURCE: Page_188]

135   cm) containing organic soil (Terreau Horticole Bio Tonusol, Agriver, France) and limestone [SOURCE: Page_188]

136   grains (Perlite Italiana Srl, Corsico, Italy) in ratio 1:1. The quantity per pot was five seeds/pot [SOURCE: Page_188]

137   for bean, ten seed/pot for wheat, one seed/pot for other plants. The term 'stres is used here [SOURCE: Page_188]

138   when, for any reason, plant performance in module growth is reduced below that achieved [SOURCE: Page_188]

139   under optimal conditions. The water supply volume for OW plants was determined during [SOURCE: Page_188]

140   preliminary experiments where we LW plants with four water levels: 30%‐, 50%‐, 70%‐ and [SOURCE: Page_188]

141   100% of soil‐saturated volume and then we measured plant parameters to find the optimal [SOURCE: Page_188]

142   conditions showing highest plant biomass and metabolism. One week after sowing, plant [SOURCE: Page_188]

143   germinations of 4‐8 cm height were transferred under laboratory conditions (23 ±2 °C, 65 ± [SOURCE: Page_188]

144   5 % RH, 16:8‐h L:D). One‐week‐old bean, wheat, milkweed, and potato were randomly [SOURCE: Page_188]

145   assigned to two water levels: 30% and 100% of soil‐saturated volumes (referred as water [SOURCE: Page_188]

146   stress and OW or 30% and 100%). The exceptional case is cabbages of which the OW was [SOURCE: Page_188]

147   70% of soil‐saturated volume because cabbage metabolism decreased at 100% volume [SOURCE: Page_188]

148   irrigation similar to the water stress symptom. The treatments were started three weeks [SOURCE: Page_188]

149   before aphid infestation and was maintained continuously. LW and OW were kept on [SOURCE: Page_188]

150   tested plants during all experiments as well. [SOURCE: Page_188]

151   Plant morphology measurements - Plant morphological parameters including height and leaf [SOURCE: Page_188]

152   mass area were measured each week. Plant height and number of nodes or stem leaves [SOURCE: Page_188]

153   were measured from the soil surface. Leaf mass per area (LMA) was calculated by dry mass [SOURCE: Page_188]

181 [SOURCE: Page_188]

154   divided by leaf area. The leaves were cut off and flattened between transparent plastic [SOURCE: Page_189]

155   leaflets and then scanned by a Sharp MX‐3140 scanner. Leaf area (cm²) was calculated using [SOURCE: Page_189]

156   the freeware ImageJ. [SOURCE: Page_189]

157   Leaf gas exchange measurement ‐ Non‐destructive measurements of leaf photosynthesis [SOURCE: Page_189]

158   rate based on net CO$_2$ assimilation (AN), transpiration rate (E) and stomatal conductance (gs) [SOURCE: Page_189]

159   were performed. We chose the 4th or 5th young leaves from the stem which provides [SOURCE: Page_189]

160   optimal aphid feeding and proved suffering water limitation long enough during preliminary [SOURCE: Page_189]

161   experiments. [SOURCE: Page_189]

162   One leaflet per plant was analyzed using a photosynthesis system (Li‐6400, Li‐Cor, Lincoln, [SOURCE: Page_189]

163   NE, USA) equipped with a light source (6200‐02B LED, LiCor). Leaflets first were acclimatized [SOURCE: Page_189]

164   in the chamber for more than 20 min under controlled conditions: leaf temperature of 27.0 [SOURCE: Page_189]

165   ± (SE) 0.1 C, CO$_2$ air concentration of 500 ± 0.04 μmol        , and saturating photosynthetic [SOURCE: Page_189]

166   photon flux density (PPFD) of 700.04 ± 0.05 μmol hν            . The light intensity of 700 µmol [SOURCE: Page_189]

167   m 2 s 1 PAR is high enough to stimulate leaf transpiration and is closer to the laboratory light [SOURCE: Page_189]

168   condition. Relative humidity was maintained between 10% and 50% in the cuvette for all [SOURCE: Page_189]

169   measurements. [SOURCE: Page_189]

170   After detecting a significant difference in at least one of morphological or metabolic [SOURCE: Page_189]

171   parameters, we transferred plants to aphid‐feeding cages. Infestation periods were [SOURCE: Page_189]

172   determined for each plant by preliminary tests. Intrinsic water use efficiency (AN/gs) was [SOURCE: Page_189]

173   calculated as the ratio between leaf photosynthesis rate (AN) and leaf stomatal conductance [SOURCE: Page_189]

174   (gs) as the study of Medrano et al. (2015). [SOURCE: Page_189]

175   Bottom-up effect of water stress on aphids [SOURCE: Page_189]

182 [SOURCE: Page_189]

176   Plant infestation – LW and OW plants were offered to aphid colonies in a cage of the [SOURCE: Page_190]

177   dimension 60x^60x^60cm covered by mesh placed in a climatic chamber (23 ±2 °C, 65 ± 5 % [SOURCE: Page_190]

178   RH, 16:8‐h L:D). We initiated new colonies by introducing 4th instar aphids into the cage of [SOURCE: Page_190]

179   OW and LW plants until the reproduction of new generations. Aphid parents were then [SOURCE: Page_190]

180   removed. Aphid colonies were maintained for 5 to 10 generations until experiments ended. [SOURCE: Page_190]

181   New plants were added every week and stayed from 1 to 2 weeks in the cages before being [SOURCE: Page_190]

182   removed. All plant metabolism and morphology were measured (see above) prior aphid [SOURCE: Page_190]

183   infestation to make sure LW plants were effectively under water stress. [SOURCE: Page_190]

184   Aphid performance: survival rate, generation time (D), and the intrinsic rate of increase (rm). [SOURCE: Page_190]

185   We evaluated aphid performance on LW and OW plants on three parameters: The survival [SOURCE: Page_190]

186   rate, the generation time (D), and the intrinsic rate of increase (rm) was calculated as [SOURCE: Page_190]

187   described by (Wyatt and White 1977; van Emden and Harrington 2007). We placed ten 4th [SOURCE: Page_190]

188   instar aphids on LW and OW plants on a Petri‐dish cage system of 85mm‐diameter covered [SOURCE: Page_190]

189   by mesh. When second aphid generations were born, we removed aphid parents and used [SOURCE: Page_190]

190   offspring aphids for evaluating aphid performance. 1‐day‐old aphids from the same cohort [SOURCE: Page_190]

191   were then individually confined into clip‐cages attached to the experimental leaves in all [SOURCE: Page_190]

192   plants from LW and OW conditions, ten clip‐cages per plant, three plants per treatment. We [SOURCE: Page_190]

193   measured the survival rate as the number of aphids surviving after seven days in clip‐cages [SOURCE: Page_190]

194   per total clip‐caged aphids. The generation time ( D) was determined by recording duration [SOURCE: Page_190]

195   from aphid to their first reproduction. The intrinsic rate of the increase relates the fecundity [SOURCE: Page_190]

196   of an individual aphid to its development time: rm = (lnMd x 0.738) / D, where Md is the [SOURCE: Page_190]

197   number of nymphs produced by the adult in the first D days of reproduction after the adult [SOURCE: Page_190]

198   molt (van Emden and Harrington 2007). [SOURCE: Page_190]

183 [SOURCE: Page_190]

199   Bottom-up effect of water stress on parasitoids [SOURCE: Page_191]

200   Parasitoid preference: no‐choice test ‐ We conducted the parasitoid specificity test as [SOURCE: Page_191]

201   previously described by Desneux et al. (2009) to test behavioral and physiological traits of [SOURCE: Page_191]

202   parasitoids on potential aphid hosts. This test design included observing the behaviour of [SOURCE: Page_191]

203   aphids and parasitoids when they individually encounter in a small area of 3‐cm diameter, 1‐ [SOURCE: Page_191]

204   cm height –dome. Parasitoids were one‐day‐emerged, mated and naive. Aphids were [SOURCE: Page_191]

205   standardized by size equal the second instar of A. pisum, the natal parasitoid host. The [SOURCE: Page_191]

206   standard size was chosen based on relative size of different aphid colonies and the [SOURCE: Page_191]

207   preferable host instar of A. ervi while parasitizing A. pisum. [SOURCE: Page_191]

208   The host selection process, i.e. female preference) was divided into 3 steps, each with a [SOURCE: Page_191]

209   perceptible representative sign: (1) detect (when the parasitoid touched the aphid either by [SOURCE: Page_191]

210   antenna or leg) – accept (when the female bent its abdomen and attacked aphid hosts, [SOURCE: Page_191]

211   following or not by aphid defense) – successful sting (when the female inserted its [SOURCE: Page_191]

212   ovipositor inside the aphid and withdrew it resulting in aphid movement). After one [SOURCE: Page_191]

213   successful sting, aphids were removed from the dome immediately for not being super [SOURCE: Page_191]

214   parasitized by A. ervi. Aphid defensive behaviors were recorded as antennal push, cornicle [SOURCE: Page_191]

215   secretion, kick or rotation (Desneux et al. 2009). All insect actions were noted as described [SOURCE: Page_191]

216   above until 5 minutes or until successful sting, depending on what happened first. A. ervi [SOURCE: Page_191]

217   females were aggressive toward aphids during our experiments, so 5‐minute length is [SOURCE: Page_191]

218   enough to complete their host selection process in a small dome. [SOURCE: Page_191]

219   Parasitoid performance: sequential dissection ‐ To follow up immature parasitoid [SOURCE: Page_191]

220   development, stung aphids from plants under limited water supply and optimal water [SOURCE: Page_191]

184 [SOURCE: Page_191]

221   supply conditions (from the previous behavioral assay, see above) were individually reared [SOURCE: Page_192]

222   in the clip cage system on leaflets belonging to their respective plant treatment, i.e. LW or [SOURCE: Page_192]

223   OW). Two third of stung aphids were then sequentially dissected to find the egg and larval [SOURCE: Page_192]

224   survival at 1‐ and 5‐day after being stung, respectively. The remaining 1/3 were kept in clip‐ [SOURCE: Page_192]

225   caged for up to 15 days, or until mummy formed, the emergence rate and the survival [SOURCE: Page_192]

226   aphids from parasitism were then recorded. We repeated the dome experiment without [SOURCE: Page_192]

227   actual behavioral observations to increase the number of replications for this assay. To [SOURCE: Page_192]

228   analyze the correlation between preference and performance for A. ervi, we considered the [SOURCE: Page_192]

229   successful stung ratio per total observed aphids as a proxy of parasitoid preference and the [SOURCE: Page_192]

230   adult emergence rate per stung aphids as a proxy of parasitoid performance. [SOURCE: Page_192]

231   Statistical analysis [SOURCE: Page_192]

232   Impact of the water limitation on plant height, plant leaf mass area and leaf gas exchange [SOURCE: Page_192]

233   was analyzed using factorial ANOVA. The impact of water stress on aphid generation time [SOURCE: Page_192]

234   and intrinsic rate of development was examined using factorial ANOVA. The impact of water [SOURCE: Page_192]

235   stress on aphid survival rate and other life history traits was analyzed using Fisher exact test. [SOURCE: Page_192]

236   Each trait was compared in pairs between LW and OW plants. The relationship between [SOURCE: Page_192]

237   parasitoid preference for and performance on aphids feeding on LW or OW plants was [SOURCE: Page_192]

238   analyzed using logistic regression with a binomial distribution. Detection, acceptance and [SOURCE: Page_192]

239   sting rates were compared, per species for each water treatment, using pairwise compared [SOURCE: Page_192]

240   using permuted Fisher Exact test with Bonferonni adjustment method. Similar analyze [SOURCE: Page_192]

241   method was used to compare the proportion of aphids containing an egg, larvae, producing [SOURCE: Page_192]

242   a mummy and adult. All tests were performed using R software (R Core team). All graphics [SOURCE: Page_192]

243   were processed using Prism 5 software (GraphPad Software Inc 2007). [SOURCE: Page_192]

185 [SOURCE: Page_192]

## 244   RESULTS

245   Impact of stress on plant morphological and metabolic traits: [SOURCE: Page_193]

246   Plant height, the number of nodes and leaf mass area (LMA) (Figure 1A, B, C) - Plant height [SOURCE: Page_193]

247   was significantly reduced under water stress for all plants tested; still, the impact was not [SOURCE: Page_193]

248   similar among plant species. Bean, milkweed, and potato showed severe height reduction [SOURCE: Page_193]

251   between OW and LW (p value > 0.05); however the number of cabbage leaves was [SOURCE: Page_193]

253   value = 0.007). There was no evidence water limitation impact on LMA of all plants (p value [SOURCE: Page_193]

254   > .05) except wheat with LMA increased by 15% (p value = 0.013). [SOURCE: Page_193]

## 296   DISCUSSION

297   In the present study, testing six aphid species as potential hosts and generating bottom‐up [SOURCE: Page_195]

298   effects on host aphids through varying water stress in host plants, we demonstrated that [SOURCE: Page_195]

299   bottom‐up effects effectively occurred on parasitoid host specificity and that it can [SOURCE: Page_195]

300   modulate its actual host range. The parasitoid choosiness was not drastically affected by [SOURCE: Page_195]

301   bottom‐up effect while host suitability for parasitoid offspring development was decreased [SOURCE: Page_195]

302   in three out of the six aphid species tested. Water stress did negatively affect plant primary [SOURCE: Page_195]

303   metabolism‐related mechanisms, e.g. leaf transpiration rate and stomatal conductance, and [SOURCE: Page_195]

304   it proved to cascade to the second trophic level, i.e. the aphids; their development speed [SOURCE: Page_195]

305   and growth were decreased for half of aphid species considered. Adverse bottom‐up effects [SOURCE: Page_195]

306   observed on aphid hosts were linked to subsequent parasitoid performance on these hosts, [SOURCE: Page_195]

307   except for sone aphid species, those showing reduced survival, generation time and/or [SOURCE: Page_195]

308   intrinsic rate of population increase (rm) proved to be suboptimal hosts for the parasitoid [SOURCE: Page_195]

309   when plants were suffering water stress. This research highlights that actual parasitoid diet [SOURCE: Page_195]

188 [SOURCE: Page_195]

310   breath observed in wild conditions largely depend on abiotic factors impacting parasitoids [SOURCE: Page_196]

311   themselves but also through less intuitive effects occurring through both host plants and [SOURCE: Page_196]

312   hosts owing to bottom‐up forces. [SOURCE: Page_196]

313   Parasitoid preference under bottom-up force [SOURCE: Page_196]

314   We demonstrated that preference traits of the aphid parasitoid A. ervi toward hosts might [SOURCE: Page_196]

315   be only slightly modulated by bottom‐up force occurring through water stress applied to [SOURCE: Page_196]

316   actual plants bearing the host aphids. No drastic difference was observed in detection, [SOURCE: Page_196]

317   acceptance and stinging rates of A. ervi when parasitizing five of the six aphid species tested [SOURCE: Page_196]

318   (R. padi, M. euphorbiae, A. nerii, A. pisum and S. avenae) on LW vs. OW plants. The host [SOURCE: Page_196]

319   selection behavior in Aphidiine aphid parasitoids relies on host‐associated cues, both from [SOURCE: Page_196]

320   the host external and internal, e.g. haemolymph quality assessed during insertion of the [SOURCE: Page_196]

321   ovipositor (Larocca et al. 2007). The present study hinted that such traits were not modified [SOURCE: Page_196]

322   in the tested aphid species, at least not enough for changing the parasitoid host selection [SOURCE: Page_196]

323   pattern. However, it could not be excluded that potential difference may occur in harsher [SOURCE: Page_196]

324   bottom‐up force conditions, i.e. that was not tested in the present study, e.g. stresses [SOURCE: Page_196]

325   inducing more variations in aphid physiology. It may also indicate that A. ervi, being an [SOURCE: Page_196]

326   intermediate generalist‐specialist, may not be adapted to detect tedious differences in [SOURCE: Page_196]

327   quality of hosts encountered. By contrast, the parasitoid behavior pattern, here detection [SOURCE: Page_196]

328   rate, was modified when parasitizing the aphid M. persicae on LW plant. Host detection step [SOURCE: Page_196]

329   by aphid parasitoid relies mostly on chemical cues on aphid cuticles (Hatano et al. 2008), [SOURCE: Page_196]

330   e.g. glucosinate (Pope et al. 2008). Water stress‐associated decrease of glucosinate [SOURCE: Page_196]

331   induction in plants (Khan et al. 2011) may translate to a reduction of this compound in M. [SOURCE: Page_196]

332   persicae cornicle secretion (Blande et al. 2008). Therefore, the decrease in M. persicae [SOURCE: Page_196]

189 [SOURCE: Page_196]

333   detection by A. ervi females may be related to such reduction of glucosinolate in the aphid [SOURCE: Page_197]

334   cornicle secretion. [SOURCE: Page_197]

335   Parasitoid performance under bottom-up force [SOURCE: Page_197]

336   Aphidius ervi showed reduced performance developing in high‐quality hosts, i.e. S. avenae, [SOURCE: Page_197]

337   M. persicae, and M. euphorbiae reared on plants suffering water stress (reduced mummy [SOURCE: Page_197]

338   and adult numbers). It did not occur when developing in its natal host, A. pisum, on which [SOURCE: Page_197]

339   the parasitoid performed well independently of the water stress applied to host plant [SOURCE: Page_197]

340   (bean). Inadequate or imbalanced nutrients could play a role in significant mortality during [SOURCE: Page_197]

341   the mummy stage of parasitoids (also evidence are scarce). Juvenile larval performance [SOURCE: Page_197]

342   depends on host size and age at parasitism, which correlates with host nutritional quality in [SOURCE: Page_197]

343   aphid parasitoids (Harvey et al. 1994; Sequeira and Mackauer 1992). In other insect families, [SOURCE: Page_197]

344   poor host food quality increases parasitoid larval mortality (Brodeur and Boivin 2004; [SOURCE: Page_197]

345   Jonasson 1994). Besides, low quality of egg and pupae as parasitoid hosts affected [SOURCE: Page_197]

346   immature parasitoid survival (Panizzi et al. 2012) or supplement proteins enhance [SOURCE: Page_197]

347   reproduction in an ectoparasitoid (Harvey et al. 2017) thus suggesting the role of host [SOURCE: Page_197]

348   protein composition in successful parasitoid development. [SOURCE: Page_197]

349          Another possible mechanism underneath may be a decrease in density of the [SOURCE: Page_197]

350   endosymbiont Buchnera in aphids feeding on low‐quality plants. The venom the female [SOURCE: Page_197]

351   parasitoid injects into its host while oviposition plays the role of aphid castration completed [SOURCE: Page_197]

352   by tetratocyte production, to redirect host nutrients to its larva; notably enhancing benefits [SOURCE: Page_197]

353   from the metabolism of the obligate aphid symbiont Buchnera toward parasitoid needs [SOURCE: Page_197]

354   (Pennacchio and Mancini 2012; Strand 2014). Moreover, there is evidence that parasitoid [SOURCE: Page_197]

190 [SOURCE: Page_197]

355   could manipulate the abundance of the primary symbiont Buchnera, from the egg and larval [SOURCE: Page_198]

356   stage (Martinez et al. 2014). [SOURCE: Page_198]

357          Parasitoid larval mortality may occur through sequestration of plant toxins via aphids [SOURCE: Page_198]

358   (Mooney et al. 2012). However, plants could both induce or decrease their toxin production [SOURCE: Page_198]

359   as a strategy to tolerate water stress. Plants then reallocate resources and increase their [SOURCE: Page_198]

360   defense via secondary metabolism, resulting in aphid host less palatable for parasitoids [SOURCE: Page_198]

361   (Mewis et al. 2012). Aphids may sequester specific toxins against their natural enemies [SOURCE: Page_198]

362   (Goodey et al. 2015) also not always occurring, e.g. increase of plant toxins may negatively [SOURCE: Page_198]

363   affect aphids without reaching parasitoids (Le Guigo et al. 2011). Among the plants used in [SOURCE: Page_198]

364   the study, cabbage, milkweed, and potato belong to the families of Brassicaceae, Asclepias, [SOURCE: Page_198]

365   and Solanaceae, respectively; all are well‐known for their constitutive toxic compounds. The [SOURCE: Page_198]

366   tissues of cabbage contain glucosinolates (sulfur derived compounds), Solanaceae contains [SOURCE: Page_198]

367   glycosidic alkaloids (tomatine, solanine) and milkweeds contain cardenolides. However, the [SOURCE: Page_198]

368   impact of water limitation on aphid performance on those plants is not similar. Different [SOURCE: Page_198]

369   mechanisms might explain the results. For the M. euphorbiae‐potato complex, the trichome [SOURCE: Page_198]

370   increases and prohibit aphids from plant feeding, therefore the mortality of aphids on LW [SOURCE: Page_198]

371   plants are relatively high (>60% under LW conditions vs. <20% under OW conditions). [SOURCE: Page_198]

372   Decreased M. persicae performance on cabbage occurred owing to known reduced plant [SOURCE: Page_198]

373   nutritional quality and increased glucosinate concentration which M. persicae tolerates only [SOURCE: Page_198]

374   at low concentration (Ali and Agrawal 2012). By contrast, the performance of A. nerii on [SOURCE: Page_198]

375   milkweed was not affected by water limitation, suggesting a tolerance to plant responses in [SOURCE: Page_198]

376   this aphid. Besides, the status of the low‐quality host of this species for A. ervi did not [SOURCE: Page_198]

377   change to a better owing to bottom‐up effect. There was a similar case for another low‐ [SOURCE: Page_198]

191 [SOURCE: Page_198]

378   quality host for A. ervi: the aphid R. padi developing on wheat did not show increased [SOURCE: Page_199]

379   suitability for A. ervi when developing on water stress plants. These occurred despite the [SOURCE: Page_199]

380   apparent negative impact of bottom‐up effect on the aphid itself; higher mortality occurred [SOURCE: Page_199]

381   likely owing to increased wheat leaf rigidity (see LMA results). [SOURCE: Page_199]

382          Finally, impairment in host metabolism regulation by the parasitoid may explain also [SOURCE: Page_199]

383   partially reduced the performance of A. ervi offspring when developing in hosts suffering [SOURCE: Page_199]

384   water‐stress bottom‐up effect. Parasitoid female could manipulate host metabolism and [SOURCE: Page_199]

385   adjust host nutritional balance to meet their larval dietary needs. The development, [SOURCE: Page_199]

386   survival, and fitness of the parasitoid offspring depend on regulatory factors they inject into [SOURCE: Page_199]

387   the host during oviposition including teratocytes, venoms, polydnaviruses and virus‐like [SOURCE: Page_199]

388   particles (Harvey et al. 2013; Poirie et al. 2009). Such disturbance may have occurred in the [SOURCE: Page_199]

389   case of A. ervi developing in M. persicae feeding on water stress plants as we observed [SOURCE: Page_199]

390   mortality at larval and mummy stages. [SOURCE: Page_199]

391          It remains unclear why A. ervi was not affected when developing in A. pisum [SOURCE: Page_199]

392   suffering water stress bottom‐up effect. Various mechanisms may explain this result. First, [SOURCE: Page_199]

393   bean plants are well tolerant to water stress thank their nitrogen fixation symbionts (Laranjo [SOURCE: Page_199]

394   et al. 2014), which induce plant nitrogen fixation and trehalose under stress. These both [SOURCE: Page_199]

395   compounds increase plant stress tolerance (Lunn et al. 2014). Second, photosynthesis rate [SOURCE: Page_199]

396   of beans was maintained under water limitation conditions, the same as wheat and potato [SOURCE: Page_199]

397   in our experiments. Differently, bean morphological palatability did not impact (like in [SOURCE: Page_199]

398   wheat which increased leaf rigidity, or potato which produced more trichomes). Third, A. [SOURCE: Page_199]

399   pisum was the natal host for A. ervi, and it may be more adapted to potential variations [SOURCE: Page_199]

400   occurring in this particular host. [SOURCE: Page_199]

192 [SOURCE: Page_199]

401   Pattern of correlation preference - performance [SOURCE: Page_200]

402   The correlation between parasitoid preference and performance at optimal water condition [SOURCE: Page_200]

403   proved that female A. ervi could detect low‐quality hosts and avoid parasitizing those in [SOURCE: Page_200]

404   some extent (in concordance with our previous results, Monticelli et al. 2018). However, [SOURCE: Page_200]

405   when the aphid‐plant systems were subjected to a bottom‐up effect, water stress in our [SOURCE: Page_200]

406   study, the preference‐performance was weakened. The capacity of host evaluation of A. ervi [SOURCE: Page_200]

407   may be disturbed when encountering hosts suffering suboptimal development conditions. It [SOURCE: Page_200]

408   hinted that the assessment made by parasitoid females when facing hosts might be mostly [SOURCE: Page_200]

409   based on fixed aphid species‐related traits rather than characteristics potentially varying as [SOURCE: Page_200]

410   a function of the aphid feeding status. We observed that there was no drastic change in A. [SOURCE: Page_200]

411   ervi behavioral pattern when tested on aphids reared on LW plants; it hints that changes in [SOURCE: Page_200]

412   A. ervi behaviors were minor, but the trend was strong enough to affect the overall female [SOURCE: Page_200]

413   capacity of optimizing diet breath as done under the optimal water supply. [SOURCE: Page_200]

414   Bottom-up effects on aphids [SOURCE: Page_200]

415   All aphids expressed in some extent tolerance to bottom‐up impacts of water stress on [SOURCE: Page_200]

416   plants, illustrated by their population stably maintained during several weeks in the [SOURCE: Page_200]

417   laboratory on both treatments. However, their life history‐traits were impacted at different [SOURCE: Page_200]

418   levels. Among six aphid species, the performance of A. nerii and A. pisum were scarcely [SOURCE: Page_200]

419   affected (it not) by water stress on plant hosts. Their ability to, well perform despite [SOURCE: Page_200]

420   bottom‐up effect may stem from two different mechanisms. A. nerii is a specialist of [SOURCE: Page_200]

421   milkweed plants and Oleander trees. The host fidelity of A. nerii is likely linked to an [SOURCE: Page_200]

422   optimized fitness on a given plant when compared to other host aphids that are for most of [SOURCE: Page_200]

193 [SOURCE: Page_200]

423   them quite polyphagous (Harrison and Mondor 2011; Smith et al. 2008). The performance [SOURCE: Page_201]

424   of A. pisum on water stress bean plants may result from the sound nutritional quality of [SOURCE: Page_201]

425   their plant hosts under the suboptimal watering conditions. Among the five plants tested, [SOURCE: Page_201]

426   only beans showed both LMA and photosynthesis not impacted by water stress. Such [SOURCE: Page_201]

427   tolerance in beans results from the mutualist protection of plant symbionts, i.e. [SOURCE: Page_201]

428   rhizobacteria and/or mycorrhizal fungi (Eldin and Moawad 1988; Nanjareddy et al. 2014). By [SOURCE: Page_201]

429   contrast, the survival rate of S. avenae, R. padi on wheat and M. euphorbiae on potato was [SOURCE: Page_201]

430   reduced under water stress. Both LW wheat and potato morphologically reduced aphid [SOURCE: Page_201]

431   feeding, as wheat LMA significantly increased as well as the density of potato trichomes [SOURCE: Page_201]

432   which entangled aphids (observed from our experiments). [SOURCE: Page_201]

433   The reduced performance of aphids on wheat, cabbage, and potato was highlighted by their [SOURCE: Page_201]

434   increased generation time and decreased the intrinsic rate of development. We supposed [SOURCE: Page_201]

435   the inadequate nutrients of plant hosts lengthened the aphid feeding time and reduced [SOURCE: Page_201]

436   their fecundity as previously shown by other studies (Han et al. 2014; Price 1991; White [SOURCE: Page_201]

437   1984; 2009). [SOURCE: Page_201]

438   Impact of water limitation on plants [SOURCE: Page_201]

439   The assays used were successful in efficiently impacting key plant traits through water [SOURCE: Page_201]

440   stress. Wheat under stress had higher LMA than OW plants. Leaf mass per area value (LMA, [SOURCE: Page_201]

441   g.m‐2) is structural plant trait reflecting leaf thickness and/or leaf density. LMA relates to the [SOURCE: Page_201]

442   plant metabolism (photosynthesis, respiratory rate), and plant vascular hydraulic transport [SOURCE: Page_201]

443   system, as well as plant growth and decomposition (de la Riva et al. 2016). LMA is [SOURCE: Page_201]

444   considered the trade‐off between plant defenses, e.g. from herbivores, by the leaf rigidity [SOURCE: Page_201]

194 [SOURCE: Page_201]

445   and the light prevention from photosynthesis and/or the plant resource allocation. Lower [SOURCE: Page_202]

446   LMA enhances conductivity within the leaf, which may facilitate photosynthesis (Poorter et [SOURCE: Page_202]

447   al. 2009). Impacted by water stress, folio cell walls are thicker. Therefore, LMA is a crucial [SOURCE: Page_202]

448   trait to predict plant adaptation strategies and/or mortality pattern under unstable [SOURCE: Page_202]

449   environments (Anderegg et al. 2016; Poorter et al. 2009). The change in leaf chemical [SOURCE: Page_202]

450   composition mediated by LMA might reflect the difference in the vascular system. LMA in [SOURCE: Page_202]

451   herbaceous plant correlates positively to leaf carbon concentration but negatively to leaf [SOURCE: Page_202]

452   nitrogen concentration, organic acids and protein concentration (de la Riva et al. 2016; [SOURCE: Page_202]

453   Poorter et al. 2009). As phloem‐ and xylem‐ feeders, insects rely on phloem available [SOURCE: Page_202]

454   nutrients (free amino acids, sugars). Plant higher LMA associated with lower N content could [SOURCE: Page_202]

455   decline aphid feeding efficiency and its quality as parasitoid host (Auclair 1963; Will et al. [SOURCE: Page_202]

456   2013). [SOURCE: Page_202]

457   The plant water transport system is a vital function in plant life. In a simplified cycle, plants [SOURCE: Page_202]

458   draw water from soil via root system, water then lost during plant transpiration in exchange [SOURCE: Page_202]

459   for CO$_2$ uptake (Sack and Scoffoni 2012). Plant mortality paradigms state that plants die [SOURCE: Page_202]

460   from either carbon starvation (carbon starvation hypothesis) or hydraulic failure (hydraulic [SOURCE: Page_202]

461   hypothesis) (Barigah et al. 2013; McDowell 2011; McDowell et al. 2008; Sevanto et al. 2014). [SOURCE: Page_202]

462   Parallelly, plants might shift their regulation between strategies of reduction of respiration [SOURCE: Page_202]

463   and/or carbon use efficiency to survive under water stress (Metcalfe et al. 2010). Those [SOURCE: Page_202]

464   accommodative behaviors in plants are classified into a spectrum of isohydric (remain leaf [SOURCE: Page_202]

465   water potentials and reduce CO$_2$ uptake via the stomatal closure) and anisohydric [SOURCE: Page_202]

466   categories (decline leaf water potential in optimizing CO$_2$ uptake) (Landsberg et al. 2017; [SOURCE: Page_202]

467   Martinez‐Vilalta et al. 2014). The stomatal closure as stress tolerance strategy consisted of [SOURCE: Page_202]

195 [SOURCE: Page_202]

468   results described by (Flexas et al. 2013; Westoby et al. 2013), the strategy might benefit [SOURCE: Page_203]

469   over leaf lifetimes. The trade‐off reduction of respiration results in the decrease in [SOURCE: Page_203]

470   photosynthesis and the deplete of carbon hydrate resources, which makes the plants more [SOURCE: Page_203]

471   susceptible to herbivores (Landsberg et al. 2017). [SOURCE: Page_203]

472   Change in plant primary metabolism and its impacts on aphid feeding. [SOURCE: Page_203]

473   During plant stress adaptation process, the plant quality as food resources for phloem [SOURCE: Page_203]

474   suckers has been shown to be impacted. Physically, water scarcity results in phloem water [SOURCE: Page_203]

475   shortage, or phloem viscosity increase and challenge aphid feeding (Landsberg et al. 2017). [SOURCE: Page_203]

476   Chemically, plant nitrogen and phosphorus content reduce (He and Dijkstra 2014), carbon [SOURCE: Page_203]

477   content (Reddy et al. 2004), increase ratio C: N which is an indicator for plant defensive [SOURCE: Page_203]

478   compound concentration (Royer et al. 2013). Higher sugar concentration in phloem and [SOURCE: Page_203]

479   weaken the immune defense of plants due to water scarcity might lead to aphid preference [SOURCE: Page_203]

480   on stressed plants. But plant poor nutrient quality (plant stress hypothesis) lead to aphids [SOURCE: Page_203]

481   smaller in size, lower in nutrient quality to bear parasitoid larvae. But the impacts depend as [SOURCE: Page_203]

482   well on aphid foraging strategies, flush or senescence feeders (White 2009, White 2015). As [SOURCE: Page_203]

483   there was no evidence of A. pisum and S. avenae belonging to senescence group (White [SOURCE: Page_203]

484   2015), we assumed they belong to flush feeders group preferring young and soft leaves as [SOURCE: Page_203]

485   the majority of insects (Price 1991). Under moderate constant water limitation, A. pisum [SOURCE: Page_203]

486   performance at the population level was reduced but not at the early stage of plants [SOURCE: Page_203]

487   (Mcvean 2001). However, no impact of water limitation is reported in S. avenae both at [SOURCE: Page_203]

488   individual and population levels on wheat (Pons and Tatchell 1995). Therefore, the effects of [SOURCE: Page_203]

489   abiotic stress on aphids are complex and simple measurements of aphid life history traits [SOURCE: Page_203]

490   could not provide high predictive results regarding adaptive trend. [SOURCE: Page_203]

196 [SOURCE: Page_203]

491   We demonstrated that the abiotic stress on plants could cascade to both second and third [SOURCE: Page_204]

492   trophic levels, even though plants and herbivores may be tolerant of long‐term water stress. [SOURCE: Page_204]

493   In overall, water stress mediated by plant hosts had an indirect adverse effect on parasitoid [SOURCE: Page_204]

494   survival and development on aphid hosts, but the impact magnitude depended on the [SOURCE: Page_204]

495   aphid‐plant complex. The interaction of plant tolerance strategies and aphid salivary protein [SOURCE: Page_204]

496   profiles might result in different attenuation of aphid host quality. The parasitoid pre‐imago [SOURCE: Page_204]

497   adaptation could also occur in its adulthood fitness under severe constant water stress. To [SOURCE: Page_204]

498   predict the diet breadth of parasitoids under constraint conditions, one should consider the [SOURCE: Page_204]

499   biological nature and the host specificity of organisms in the micro‐ecosystem as well as the [SOURCE: Page_204]

500   stress profile, such as stress timing, frequency, duration, and intensity. For further research, [SOURCE: Page_204]

501   the effects of moderate water stress or pulsed stress on the same tri‐trophic complex [SOURCE: Page_204]

502   should also be considered as plant reactions under different stress scenarios are proved to [SOURCE: Page_204]

503   be very different, and it may cascade toward various outcomes in the wild. [SOURCE: Page_204]

Codes of stress type: all ecological = A+B; T: thermal (cold/hot); CO$_2$ = C; Abiotic = A; cid : toxins/pesticides; B: biotic. ## GENERAL DISCUSSION

The use of biological control agents: BCA that are natural enemies of agricultural pests requires a deep understanding of their ecological specialization. BCA preference and performance on specific hosts/preys determine their pest suppression efficacy. Their host fidelity predicts their ability to switch from one targeted host to another non‐targeted species. Their diet breadth predicts the ability to implement and maintain their presence in a new environment. These parameters depend on the BCA host specificity, which is furthermore influenced by ecological forces. [SOURCE: Page_254]

Aphid parasitoids, one common BCA were the model in this study. Their larvae develop inside the aphid host of which the adults emerge. The host bodies, then, represent both food resource and environment for larva parasitoid development. The host choice of female adults is the dead‐or‐living matter for the larvae. On encountering a host, the female adult should decide to lay eggs or not, and the sex ratio of their offspring. It seems that parasitoid mothers know what is best for their descendants and their species as a whole. Understanding factors leading to such preference is therefore essential. The parasitoid strategies of specialization could focus on plant or aphid resources because both aphid and plant host quality could impact parasitoid performance. Some parasitoids, for instance, specialize in phylogenetically closed aphids. Others could specialize on plant species regardless of aphid phylogeny. [SOURCE: Page_254]

This study focused on the factors modulating the host specificity of aphid parasitoids within the plant‐aphid‐parasitoid tri‐trophic system in the laboratory. These factors are both biotic and abiotic, i.e. plant‐, aphid‐ and parasitoid traits as well as environmental stress, more especially soil water limitation. The first goal was to assess the fundamental host specificity of three parasitoids by the host range testing method on 14 aphid species. Also, their degree of host specificity was evaluated with the host specificity index and the relationship preference‐performance. The discrimination between parasitoid specialists and generalists vaguely mentioned in literature has been clarified on a subtle scale in this study. The second goal was to assess the impact of water limitation on the fundamental host specificity of one parasitoid, i.e. on their preference and performance and their optimal foraging pattern. [SOURCE: Page_254]

247 [SOURCE: Page_254]

First of all, we showed that our three parasitoids have different fundamental host specificity. One parasitoid (A. ervi) is an intermediate specialist and mainly parasitizes aphids from the Macrosiphini tribe. The others, D. rapae, and A. abdominalis are generalists and parasitize a phylogenetically broad range of aphid hosts. Their behaviors differed: A. ervi showed a preference for hosts on which they performed well, but D. rapae and A. abdominalis did not. The preference‐performance correlation relates to a lower host range for A. ervi and a smaller host specificity index. In other words, specialist parasitoids are more selective in host choice if they specialize in a few hosts and is called the optimal foraging pattern. As the three parasitoids are often considered host generalists in literature, the host specificity index and the optimal foraging strategy are efficient ways to classify specialist/generalist species on a fine scale. [SOURCE: Page_255]

This study on host specificity confirms the general statement that A. ervi is an intermediate specialist, not sufficiently proved in previous literature, of the Macrosiphini tribe. However, among Macrosiphini, A. ervi was unable to parasitize M. dirhordum, either because it is protected by its bacterial symbiont Hamiltonella defensa or because the A. ervi strain in this study did not genetically adapt to M. dirhordum. Biotic or biogeographical factors might be able to modulate the host range. The difference between the parasitoid field host range and the fundamental host range implies the impact of environmental and geographical factors to parasitoid specialization. Future research should focus on the calculation of field host specificity index for a wide range of BCA, as in the Skoracka and Kuczyński study (2012). Moreover, the host specificity index would provide a broader and deeper view on the potential phylogenetic signal of parasitoid species on their host specificity (Derocles et al. 2016) as the phylogenetic relationship may correlate to similar traits in host species (Guénard et al. 2013). Furthermore, we could study the characteristics of intermediate specialist/generalists, for example, whether the correlation preference‐performance only exists for intermediate specialist/generalist but not for extreme specialists or generalists? [SOURCE: Page_255]

Secondly, water limitation was proved to affect parasitoid efficiency, specifically A. ervi. Both their preference and performance on aphid hosts were adversely impacted, however asymmetrically. In most cases, A. ervi preference on aphid hosts was not significantly reduced except the detection rate when they encountered the aphid M. persicae. However, their [SOURCE: Page_255]

248 [SOURCE: Page_255]

performance on good or suboptimal quality hosts decreased considerably. These asymmetrical modifications in life‐history traits of A. ervi led to the loss of the correlation preference‐performance. Differently speaking, their host selection efficiency was impacted; parasitoids mother no longer knew which best hosts for their offspring were. For further study, we should test A. ervi behavior in choice‐conditions to see if learning could improve their capacity of evaluating low‐quality hosts. [SOURCE: Page_256]

This study also showed a diversity of responses to water stress at both trophic levels, plant, and aphid, resulting in different impacts on the third trophic level, parasitoid. Plants react differently to water regimes. For example, optimal water supply for cabbages was 70% field capacity while it was 100% for all the other plants. The 100% soils saturated volume cause stress symptom on cabbages similar to water limitation (consistent with Khan et al. 2010). Interestingly, parasitoid performance was more efficient on 70% cabbages (chapter 5, article 3) compare to 100% (chapter 4, article 1), strengthening the preference‐performance correlation under optimal environmental conditions. Water limitation might also influence plant physical and/or chemical defenses. In this study, aphid survival rate significantly decreased in water‐stressed wheat with harden structure (increased LMA) and Glas et al. (2012) shown a negative impact on aphid of the increased of trichome density in potato. [SOURCE: Page_256]

The resistance of aphid A. pisum to the bottom‐up effect of water stress and A. ervi performance on such aphids are interesting. Why could not the bottom‐up impact reach the top‐level like in the other plant‐aphid combinations? One hypothesis suggests a positive effect of the symbiosis between Rhizobium and fava beans that buffer the bottom‐up effect at higher trophic levels. Also, whether the advantage of natal hosts on A. ervi could buffer impacts of plant water stress? What happens if A. ervi are reared on a different host species but not A. pisum? For further study, one could test A. ervi on natal host S. avenae and compare their performance on S. avenae and A. pisum under similar water limitation. [SOURCE: Page_256]

In parasitoids, the mortality at egg stage is caused by host immune defenses or symbionts, at larva stage by plant toxins sequestrated by aphids and at the nymphal stage by low nutritional quality (Desneux et al. 2009a). Regardless of insecticidal compounds of plant hosts or host quality, A. ervi mortality happened mostly from the nymphal stage but not earlier. A. ervi late mortality inside aphid hosts probably because they have a placenta‐like structure of eggs with [SOURCE: Page_256]

249 [SOURCE: Page_256]

a thick cellular wall, which helps the egg to second instar larva to drive the nutrients efficiently (Sabri et al. 2011). This structure is unique to A. ervi until now (Martinez et al. 2016) and probably confer resistance to A. ervi larva even when they develop on aphid hosts feeding on toxic plant hosts like A. nerii. It could be interesting to test the hypothesis on other parasitoids to see their pattern of mortality inside aphid hosts. Nitrogen‐deprived compounds in aphids might have high impacts on the parasitoid larval development [SOURCE: Page_257]

Diet breadth of both aphids and parasitoids seems to affect their efficiency of host exploiting, as predicted by the “trade‐off host range breadth – host use efficiency” model (Straub et al. 2011). Myzus persicae, an extreme generalist aphid, was severely suffered from water stress as well as parasitoid parasitizing them. The trade‐off model could be investigated further at aphids, that is to say, whether more specialist aphids could resist better under bottom‐up impacts of water stress. One could equally examine other parasitoids share the same hosts with A. ervi but have different diet breadth like Aphidius rhopalosiphi, a so‐called specialist on wheat aphids, or Aphelinus abdominalis, a generalist, to compare the magnitude of bottom‐up impacts. [SOURCE: Page_257]

Implication in biological control and ecology [SOURCE: Page_257]

Overall, this evidence of the bottom‐up effect of soil water limitation on parasitoid host specificity raises a concern for parasitoids as BCA. Both plants and aphids can tolerate a moderate, long‐term water limitation, even though their performance were negatively impacted. Aphid populations could be maintained on water stress plants for several weeks without extinction in this study. However, the parasitoid mortality on aphid hosts significantly increased, such as from 60% under optimal water supply to 94.5% under water limitation on M. persicae hosts. Therefore, the top trophic level with high degree of food specialization like parasitoids might suffer more than the lower trophic levels. Further studies should be done to confirm this statement, for example investigating parasitoid population dynamic on aphid hosts feeding on water‐stressed plants. [SOURCE: Page_257]

In case of confirmed negative bottom‐up impacts of abiotic factors on parasitoids, should one release these BCA under abiotic constraints? That could be an economic loss. In the field application, practitioners could increase parasitoid host selection effectiveness by methods [SOURCE: Page_257]

250 [SOURCE: Page_257]

like (1) previous learning, such as exposing them to low‐quality hosts before or by inducing volatile plant compounds to locate hosts; (2) application of a mix of natural enemies, e.g. specialist BCA on shared host aphids; (3) use of banker plant‐aphid combination that are resistant to abiotic stress to support BCA. The responses of organisms from their morphology to metabolism and proteome profiles are very different for each kind of water stress or stress combination, e.g. the intensity, duration, and timing (Huberty and Denno 2004; White 2009; Price 1991). It could be interesting to test all kinds of water limitation regimes or combined abiotic stress, e.g. water limitation and fertilizer supply on similar tri‐trophic systems to optimize water regimes. [SOURCE: Page_258]

Prospects for risk and efficiency assessments of BCA [SOURCE: Page_258]

From this study, we highlighted the importance of parasitoid capacity of adaptation under field conditions, especially on current climate change era. This capacity is called parasitoid plasticity. We also emphasized the necessity of both efficiency and risk assessments of BCA, where parasitoid specificity could predict parasitoid plasticity. We shared the same view with Heimpel (2017) of raising acceptable risk levels of non‐targeted species for BCA. Zero‐risk parasitoids could fail in the field in considering the trade‐off between specialist and generalist, specificity and plasticity. Firstly, the more specialist parasitoids could result in the less productive BCA on the disturbed environment. Secondly, risk assessment in laboratory conditions could result in overestimating the ecological risks of the BCA and pretermit good BCA. Thirdly, invasive species are continuously introduced into new areas following human activities; the ecological risks are nonetheless unavoidable. The requirement of non‐risk BCA could delay the process of new BCA approval and BC practices in general. However, this shifting paradigm requires cautious improvement of risk and efficiency the assessment. Notably, we suggested several ideas of specificity and plasticity evaluations as following. Methods of host specificity evaluation could be broadened to find correlation among parasitoid traits in combining with different indexes. For example, we could study the set of criteria of host specificity index and correlation performance‐host phylogeny, or the set of host specificity index and ovigeny index. Concerning parasitoid plasticity, it should be considered multidimensional concept similar to specificity. The degree of plasticity could be different to each dimension. Parasitoid behavioral plasticity could be understood as the low [SOURCE: Page_258]

251 [SOURCE: Page_258]

choosiness towards aphid hosts. Determinants of generalist behaviors in parasitoids could be innate, such as parasitoid time, energy and egg constraints; or through interactions like host deprivation/availability, competition. Determinants of parasitoid physiological plasticity could underly in their venom compositions. Therefore a comparison of venom compositions of parasitoids reared on different hosts over several generations could be interesting. The study of parasitoid plasticity to disturbed conditions should play vital roles in BCA efficiency assessments. Determinants of stress tolerance at both aphid and parasitoid levels should be highlighted. In aphids, this information serves to understand aphid prevalence, in parasitoids to choose relevant BCA. These determinants could be surprisingly similar in both parasitoids and aphids, e.g. multi‐dimensional specificity could impact plasticity: generalist vs. extreme specialist parasitoid, time‐limited vs. egg‐limited, habitat vs. host. The types of stressors such as nutrients or soil salinity could determine the defensive and adaptive strategies of all trophic levels. The frequency, intensity, and duration of stressors: pulsed vs. continuous, high vs. medium, long‐term vs. short‐term could drive the outcomes of plasticity. [SOURCE: Page_259]

This study of factors modulating the host specificity of aphid parasitoids not only suggest the significant roles of abiotic constraints on BCA but also contributing to more extensive ecological understanding and theory on parasitoid host specialization. [SOURCE: Page_259]

252 [SOURCE: Page_259]
